## 3 Joseph A. Schumpeter

## An economist between Marx and Walras

### 3.1 Introduction

Schumpeter was a major social scientist, and remains so in the sense that his personality and his work continue their impact through his students, many of whom succeeded in joining the vanguard of modern economics; and through his books and essays, that have not lost any of their force as works of reference and as sources of ideas. There are several societies that carry his name, among them the International Joseph A. Schumpeter Society, the Vienna Schumpeter Gesellschaft, and the Graz Schumpeter Society. His likeness has never appeared on a banknote, as did that of his teacher Eugen von Böhm-Bawerk on a 100 Shilling banknote, but such honours are transient. Until quite a short time ago it was true that in the University of Graz, his early place of work, 'one would look in vain for any sign commemorating one of its greatest scholars' (Seidl 1982: 52). This has since changed, and even in the city the alert passer-by will now see a commemorative plaque at Parkstraße 17, where Schumpeter once lived.

In this chapter I will focus above all on those writings on which Schumpeter worked while he was in Graz or which were either published during that period or shortly afterwards. I will also make some brief comments on some of his other writings closely related to these. Such a constraint is only prudent, for Schumpeter's complete works are so voluminous that an essay such as this cannot seriously cover them all; while it also coincides with what Schumpeter himself said of the third decade of a scholar's life: it is 'the decade of sacred fertility'.

A word about the choice of title: 'Joseph A. Schumpeter: an economist between Marx and Walras'. The names of Karl Marx and Léon Walras mark the antipodes between which Schumpeter's writing and thinking move. Of course, he was also subject to many more influences, among them the Austrian teachers of whom he thought so much. (An esteem that was not always reciprocated, as we shall see.) But the names of Walras and Marx seem to me especially suited to a tension between the centripetal forces of economic equilibrium and the centrifugal forces that upset this equilibrium; leading forces which, in Schumpeter's view, were characteristic of capitalist economies. One could exaggerate and say that Schumpeter began as an undisciplined Walrasian and ended as a disciplined Marxian. He started out valuing the Walrasian system of general equilibrium more than anything else, while recognising that it was not capable of containing the forces and anarchic tendencies of the system. He ended up as a prophet of the rise of socialism, not through revolutionary change as Marx had supposed, but rather more peacefully, as the outcome of a slow and gradual process of socio-economic transformation. The purposively-oriented behaviour of agents brought about results they had neither planned, nor foreseen: 'History is the result of human action, but not the execution of any human design' (Ferguson 1793: 205).<sup>1</sup>

{30}------------------------------------------------

Schumpeter's work deserves attention, and the best form of such attention is its critical analysis. Schumpeter wanted to be the best in practically everything he did in life. His ambition knew no bounds. I will not therefore avoid consideration of how his aspirations compared with his actions and those of others, against whom he measured himself.

I begin with a survey of Schumpeter's life, his academic career and the major private, political and economic setbacks that he suffered, and which so badly affected the early successes of the boy wonder, the Wunderkind (Section 3.2). Following this, I turn to some of his most significant writings that were completed during his early life and during the period that ended with his resignation from his post in Graz. I will also deal with his later intellectual development, which echo so much of what he had already done by the time that he left Graz (Section 3.3). So far as his fundamental ideas are concerned, there is an astonishing continuity throughout Schumpeter's work. From the beginning, it was the problem of 'economic development' that gained his closest attention – the question of 'how the economic system creates the forces that constantly transform it' (Schumpeter 1964: XXII).<sup>2</sup> In the 'Introduction' to the first edition of *Theorie der wirtschaftlichen Entwicklung* – which was published as early as 1911 – we can read that 'it was always one and the same basic idea to which I directed myself, and this basic idea concerns on the one hand the entire domain of theory, and on the other permits the markers of theoretical knowledge of the tendency of the phenomenon of economic development to be pushed forward, a good deal' (Schumpeter 1912: VI). What Schumpeter says here about his early writings can be applied to his entire oeuvre. In the final section I deal briefly with the influence of Schumpeter's work.

### 3.2 Biography<sup>3</sup>

#### 3.2.1 Childhood and Youth

Joseph Aloisius Julius Schumpeter was born on 8 February 1883 to Josef, a cloth manufacturer and merchant, and Johanna (maiden name Grüner) in Triesch, Moravia, then part of the Austro-Hungarian Empire and today known as Třešt, Czech Republic. Following the premature death of his father, Joseph and his twenty-seven-year-old mother moved in 1888 to Graz and were taken under the wing of Johanna's later husband, Field Marshall (retired) Sigismund von Kéler. In Graz Schumpeter attended the Elementary School. In 1893 the family moved to Vienna, where the social position of his aristocratic stepfather enabled Schumpeter to enter the Theresianum, the elite school of the aristocracy, the Eton of Austro-Hungary (Swedberg 1991: 11). Here the pupil learned among other things several languages, fencing, riding and 'his agreeable, sometimes quaintly overpolite old-world manner' (Haberler 1950: 335). The eight years in the Theresianum left a clear mark on the young man: he was said to be overbearing, arrogant, lacking in seriousness and opportunistic. His friend Felix Somary testified that 'he never took anything in life seriously'. In 1901 he completed his studies at the Theresianum and in the same year entered the University of Vienna. He had decided to study economics. At that time this was only possible within the framework of the study of law; he also attended lectures in mathematics and statistics.

Economic theory was his principal interest. In Austria the work of Carl Menger, together with his pupils Eugen von Böhm-Bawerk and Friedrich von Wieser, had contributed to a significant rise in interest in the subject; moreover, they had during the Methodenstreit laid into Gustav Schmoller and the Younger Historical School with great success and made a stand for pure theory. In 1903 Menger formally retired, and his chair passed to Wieser. Schumpeter studied principally under Wieser as well as Eugen von Philippovich, the author of the leading textbook of the period; from 1904 he also studied under Böhm-Bawerk. He wrote assignments which in revised form found their way into his first major study of the history of theory in 1914 (Schumpeter 1914a).<sup>4</sup> Schumpeter mixed with ultraliberals, socialists and Marxists – among them Ludwig von Mises, Emil Lederer, Felix Somary, Otto Bauer and Rudolf Hilferding. They discussed Marx's theory of value and distribution in particular, Böhm-Bawerk having made a frontal assault on this on the occasion of the posthumous publication of the third volume of *Das Kapital*; Hilferding's 1904 essay in defence of Marx; as well as

{31}------------------------------------------------

the socialist alternative to capitalism. These discussions were to find a lasting echo in Schumpeter's writings.

#### 3.2.2 Czernowitz and Graz

During the summer of 1905 Schumpeter sat his final law exams, and at the beginning of 1906 his finals in legal history and public administration. In February 1906 he was awarded his doctorate in law. Directly following this he went travelling. First he went to Berlin, where he attended Gustav Schmoller's seminar; then he went to France and England. He stayed for a year in London and registered as a Research Student at the London School of Economics. At the end of 1907 he married Gladys Ricarde Seaver. In 1907 he moved to Cairo to practise at the International Court. During this period he completed *Das Wesen und der Hauptinhalt der theoretischen Nationalökonomie* [The Nature and the Substance of Theoretical Economics] and from Cairo sent it off to be published – it appeared the following year (Schumpeter 1908).

Back again in Vienna, in October 1908 he presented his book as a second dissertation ('Habilitation') to the Law and Social Sciences Faculty so that he might gain the right to teach. In March 1909 he was duly registered as a Privatdozent. In the autumn of that year he was appointed to an associate professorship at the University of Czernowitz, then capital of the principality of Bukovina and a part of the Austro-Hungarian Empire. Here, in surroundings that offered little in the way of intellectual stimulation, but quietude, Schumpeter wrote his *Theorie der wirtschaftlichen Entwicklung* [Theory of Economic Development], generally regarded as his magnum opus. The book appeared in 1911 (although the imprint states 1912), the year in which Schumpeter returned to Graz as a full Professor of Political Economy. Many of the ideas contained in the book had been trailed the year before in his essay 'Über das Wesen der Wirtschaftskrisen [On the Nature of Economic Crises]' (Schumpeter 1910). Here he advanced a conception of crisis not as a sudden interruption of business, disrupting the continuity of economic development, but as a necessary phase of the business cycle.<sup>5</sup> What he seeks is an explanation of periodic economic expansion, how this propagates, why this expansion does not simply continue unchecked but peaks in a crisis leading into a downturn, why this recession is in turn not permanent, but sooner or later arrives at a turning point leading into an upturn from which a new phase of expansion begins. In this perspective the crisis is not a symptom of a breakdown in the system, but an intrinsic component of the normal developmental process of capitalist economies.<sup>6</sup> Schumpeter had already differentiated 'static' from 'dynamic' perspectives in his *Wesen und Hauptinhalt*, and made clear that crises and cycles can only be understood from the latter perspective. In the essay, and then again in *Theorie*, he repeats this view and sets himself apart from the supposedly thoroughly statical perspective of Walras.<sup>7</sup>

Schumpeter's appointment to Graz met with bitter opposition from Richard Hildebrand (the son of Bruno Hildebrand, the noted 'older' German historical economist) who kept the flag (or perhaps pennant) of historicism flying in Graz and sought to rally forces against everything that looked like economic theory. As Chairman of the Appointments Committee he even went so far as to claim that Schumpeter's *Wesen* contained nothing but 'empty generalities' and 'trivialities'; Hildebrand's outpourings ended with the judgement: 'Up to the present, he has yet to produce anything that could remotely be described as scholarly work' (see Seidl 1984: 194, fn. 47). Against Hildebrand's recommendation, and that of the greater part of the primarily nationalistic German professoriate, Schumpeter was appointed full Professor of Political Economy in Graz on 30 October 1911 with the Emperor's 'most definite resolve'.<sup>8</sup> But Schumpeter's early time in Graz was not easy: students boycotted his lectures and seminars on the grounds that they thought he asked too much of them.

Schumpeter began teaching in Graz in September 1912, and the turbulence associated with his appointment initially subsided. He was an excellent teacher, taking on an enormous teaching load in the university, and later at the Technical College. Two years after his appointment he went on

{32}------------------------------------------------

exchange to Columbia University, New York. His teaching there likewise met with great success, and brought him into contact with leading American economists such as Irving Fisher, Frank Taussig and Wesley Clair Mitchell. Some of these would turn out to be of importance for the continuation of his career. He returned to Graz; his wife refused to accompany him and returned to England, ending the marriage so far as Schumpeter was concerned. In Graz Schumpeter was elected Dean of the Law Faculty for the academic year 1916–17. He was, however, politically isolated. His activities during the later part of the decade, first of all in the German 'Socialisation Commission' and then as Minister of Finance, sharpened conflicts with his Graz colleagues. In 1918 he threw his support behind the appointment of Arthur Spiethoff to the new second chair, but in the confusion of the monarchy's collapse nothing came of this.

From 1916 Schumpeter undertook various peace initiatives and offered his counsel to Emperor Charles I in Vienna. He did this among other things with a series of informal memoranda (see Schumpeter 1985; Seidl 1982; Swedberg 1991, Ch. 3, section I; and above all Hedtke 2004).<sup>9</sup> It seems that Spiethoff, then Professor in Prague, drew to the attention of Schumpeter the contents of a secret appendix to a diplomatic note of November 1915 addressed to the government in Vienna in which a customs union between Germany and Austro-Hungary was proposed. Schumpeter vigorously opposed this proposal, arguing that Austria was in danger of becoming a German colony.<sup>10</sup> In a later memorandum he dealt with the possibilities of restoring Austria's multinational character to check resurgent nationalism linked to peace negotiations after the World War, as well as the prospect of founding a political party based on the aristocracy and the bourgeoisie to secure a transition to a new conservative epoch.<sup>11</sup>

During the winter of 1918–19 and following a proposal by two of his Marxist acquaintances, Rudolf Hilferding and Emil Lederer, Schumpeter was appointed to the Socialisation Commission established by the new German Social Democrat government. Under the chairmanship of Karl Kautsky the Commission considered whether the German coal industry should be socialised as the first of a number of industries. In February 1919 the provisional findings were reported to the government; remarkably, the document was also signed by Schumpeter. According to the testimony of members of the Commission, Schumpeter was in favour of the project of socialising the coal industry, a stance that astonished Rudolf Hilferding.

In 1918, Schumpeter published his study on 'The Crisis of the Tax State' which dealt with the problems of war finance and the consequent difficulties of restoring state finances (Schumpeter 1918). In this essay Schumpeter expressly warned against treating socialisation as a means of resolving Austria's current problems. Early in 1919 Schumpeter had a long-cherished wish fulfilled: he was appointed Secretary of State for Finance in the cabinet of Karl Renner (see März 1979: 73–6). This appointment was not universally welcomed, and he was confronted with a wave of mistrust. The press mocked his lack of consistency. In the *Fackel* Karl Kraus, alluding to his visiting appointment at Columbia University, dubbed him 'Professor of Opinion Exchange'. While this line of criticism first emerged in public, it soon became heard within the cabinet itself. Schumpeter was politically independent and quickly came into conflict with the leading representatives of the coalition parties, the Social Democrats and the Christian Conservatives, and especially with his former fellow student Otto Bauer, now Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs. Conflict flared over Schumpeter's fierce opposition to the policy of federation with Germany (*Anschluß*) which the government was pursuing. He attacked Bauer in public on this matter, and went on to brief a British financial expert on an economic plan that had initially been kept secret from some of his cabinet colleagues. Moreover, his opponents accused him of profiting from the sale to the Italian Fiat group in the summer of 1919 of a majority shareholding in Alpine-Montan AG, the largest Austrian heavy industrial firm. In this way he derailed the socialisation of both firm and industry, since the Italians, as victors, would not agree to such a move. Schumpeter came under increasing pressure from his cabinet colleagues, especially from the Social Democrats. Ironically, the plan he had devised to

{33}------------------------------------------------

reorganise state finances was published on the very same day (17 October 1919) that he was forced to resign, just seven months after his appointment. The plan was never implemented.

#### 3.2.3 Vienna

Schumpeter's political involvement had resulted in his neglecting his university post in Graz. Yet again he sought a relaxation of his teaching duties and in this way provoked irritation among both his colleagues and his students. Not until the summer semester of 1920 did he once more teach in Graz. From May 1921 the University granted him unpaid leave. In October 1921 he finally gave up his post for good. Following his lack of success in politics he now sought his fortune in the private sector, and was appointed President of the bank Biedermann & Co. in July 1921.<sup>12</sup> Schumpeter's involvement in politics had cost him a great deal, and his flamboyant lifestyle was also expensive. He borrowed money from the bank and invested in several firms. At first things went well, and he made a great deal of money. He lived the high life and was quite Jack the Lad in Vienna society. He was happy to be seen in the company of a variety of prostitutes. This all came to a sudden end with the Austrian economic crisis of 1924. It took only a few weeks for Schumpeter to lose everything. Among other things he was cheated financially by another former pupil of the Theresianum, and the bank, now in deep trouble itself, sacked him. Heavily in debt, he was fired from the post of President on 11 September 1924. This was the second debacle within only a few years that he had to deal with. Nor did he have a teaching post to return to any more, where he could lick his wounds and collect himself. Things did not look good for our mastermind! During the following years he worked his way out of debt as a jobbing journalist and speaker.

#### 3.2.4 Bonn

Schumpeter was lucky – he still had friends and admirers. One of these was Arthur Spiethoff.<sup>13</sup> Now professor in Bonn, Spiethoff managed to get Schumpeter appointed in October 1925 to a chair in Economics at his university. Schumpeter applied for German citizenship and moved to Bonn. Spiethoff was a student of Schmoller but nonetheless an enlightened historicist; nevertheless he asked Schumpeter to teach anything he liked, but not theory. At first Schumpeter adhered to this request. He had great success with students, who streamed from far and wide to Bonn. He had a unique ability to weave a spell over those who attended his lectures. According to Erich Schneider, one of his students, it was solely on account of Schumpeter that within a few years Bonn turned into a Mecca for the study of economics. Schumpeter found himself again, and within a short time published a number of influential articles.<sup>14</sup> He started work on a new and major topic: a theory of money.

Something else refreshed his mood and gave him encouragement. On 5 November 1925, he married Anna Josefina Reisinger, a woman some 20 years younger than Schumpeter. She was the daughter of the caretaker of his mother's apartment building in Vienna, and he had known her for years. He took her to Bonn with him and they shared a very happy period. Shortly following his marriage Anna became pregnant; but fate waited in the shadows. His much-loved mother died on 22 June 1926. His first wife Gladys, who had in the meantime found out about his remarriage, threatened to take him to court for bigamy. Then the worst happened: on 3 August his wife died in labour, and shortly afterwards also the son to which she had given birth. Over several years he did not touch his wife's room. He also developed a personal death cult, talking every day to his mother and wife.

He sought to smother pain and mourning by throwing himself into his work. In 1926 a completely revised second edition of his *Theorie* was published. One of the important changes to his previous argument was reflected in an article published in the *Economic Journal* two years later – 'The Instability of Capitalism' (Schumpeter 1928). In this article he described his understanding of capitalism's inherent self-destructive tendencies and the forces which effected its transcendence; this anticipated one of the major themes of his book *Capitalism, Socialism and Democracy*,

{34}------------------------------------------------

published some fifteen years later (Schumpeter 1942). In the essay Schumpeter also took account of the fact that the much-admired figure of the 'entrepreneur' was becoming ever rarer. Competitive capitalism was being replaced by cartelised, organised capitalism. This was characterised by the separation of ownership from control and the increased importance of a new caste of managers. The older relations and structures were being completely overturned, steamrolled by Taylorisation, routinisation and bureaucratisation; quite unwittingly the path was prepared for a 'March into Socialism'.<sup>15</sup>

Schumpeter would never complete his new project on the theory of money. The appearance of John Maynard Keynes's *Treatise on Money* in 1930 appeared to entirely undermine it, and Schumpeter played with the idea of simply destroying his manuscript.<sup>16</sup> From autumn 1927 to early 1928, and then again towards the end of 1930, Schumpeter was Visiting Professor in the Harvard Department of Economics. Together with Ragnar Frisch and others he was a founding member of the Econometric Society, and was for many years on its Committee – even President from 1940 to 1941. His return trip to Germany in 1931 went by way of Japan, where he gave several lectures that were well-received.<sup>17</sup> Back in Bonn once more he showed some interest in an appointment in Berlin as successor to Sombart or Herkner, but he was passed over.

This setback shook Schumpeter deeply. How could he demonstrate to his German colleagues the error of their ways? He let his American friends in Harvard know that he no longer was disinclined to entertain the idea of moving there, even if on a temporary basis initially. In May 1932 he let it be known that he had decided to accept an appointment at Harvard.

#### 3.2.5 Harvard

In the autumn of 1932 Schumpeter moved to Cambridge, Massachusetts. For the time being he left most of his possessions and property in Europe. He lived in the house of his academic admirer and fatherly friend Frank Taussig until he married Elizabeth Boody Firuski in the summer of 1937. He managed to negotiate a good salary for himself and with this was able by 1935 to pay off his old debts. In 1939 he became an American citizen. Once again, Schumpeter was extremely successful as a teacher. Some of the most brilliant minds were attracted to his lectures and seminars, among them Paul Samuelson (who called Schumpeter 'a great showman'), James Tobin, Richard Musgrave, Abram Bergson, Richard Goodwin, Paul Sweezy and Kenneth Galbraith. Schumpeter proposed a lecture course in mathematical economics and ran it himself until he passed it over to Wassily Leontief, who had in the meantime arrived at Harvard via Berlin, Kiel and China. Schumpeter's colleagues were not that impressed by his mathematical ability, but they approved of the initiative.

To Schumpeter's great vexation Keynes had become world famous and his latest book, the *General Theory of Employment, Interest and Money* (1936), was enthusiastically taken up by very many teachers and students. Only a few refused to join the enthusiasts, among them Schumpeter. He made heavy weather of the Englishman's success, all the more so as Galbraith, Alvin Hansen and finally also Samuelson became converts to Keynesianism, spreading the word in textbooks and helping to make a version of it a world-wide success. Schumpeter did not bother to conceal his disgruntlement and published a review whose contempt could scarcely be exceeded (Schumpeter 1936). He accused Keynes of sharing the 'Ricardian Vice', deducing far-reaching policy conclusions from trivial models; suggested that there were a number of other, although unspecified, flaws; approved of his monetary theory of interest on the grounds that for one thing 'it follows on from mine'; and concluded by suggesting that, 'The less said about this book, the better' (Schumpeter 1987 [1936]: 84).<sup>18</sup>

Keynes had stolen a march on him once more. For Schumpeter, it was like being the hare in the fairy story about the tortoise and the hare. He desperately sought some way to respond. He worked without regard to his health and in 1939 published *Business Cycles*, two volumes and more than a thousand pages (Schumpeter 1939). In this book he broadened his perspective upon the dynamics

{35}------------------------------------------------

of the capitalist economy, and specified in greater detail his conception of the basic underlying processes and effective reinforcing mechanisms, discussed the interaction of cycles of differing interval and amplitude that he thought overlaid each other, and sought to support the findings with a wealth of empirical material. But the book appeared at an unpropitious moment, and success eluded him. It was harshly criticised by Simon Kuznets in the *American Economic Review* (1940). Schumpeter's dream of being the greatest economist in the world lay in ruins.

A few years earlier in 1934 an English edition of the recently revised *Theorie* was published (Schumpeter 1934), the revisions anticipating some of the changes vis-à-vis his earlier views that would appear in *Business Cycles*. While Schumpeter had in the first edition of *Theorie* drawn upon Juglar and assumed that cycles lasted from nine to ten years, he introduced in *Theory of Economic Development* and then later in *Business Cycles* an additional long wave of around 50 years. Here he relied especially on the Russian analyst Nikolai Kondratiev, and on Spiethoff. He identified three such long waves leading up to the First World War, each of which was initiated by fundamental technical breakthroughs (later called 'basic innovations'): the 'first Kondratiev' triggered by the steam engine (1787–1842); the second by the railway (1843–1897); and the third by electrification (1897–c.1940). Alongside the very long Kondratievs and medium Juglars there were, as Schumpeter argued in *Business Cycles*, also shorter forty month inventory cycles.

Kuznets's review acknowledged the enormous achievement that *Business Cycles* represented, but took issue with several of Schumpeter's theses, and two in particular: that innovations arise abruptly and in clusters; and that there are several types of cycle that overlay each other. In particular, Kuznets doubted the existence of Kondratiev's. Schumpeter's statistical methods were far too primitive to provide appropriate support for his thesis. At the worst, Schumpeter was vulnerable to the argument that he had ultimately not been able to demonstrate a convincing reason for the endogeneity of cycles. As Kuznets commented ironically, everything depended on the idea that after every fifty years the heroic entrepreneurial characters got tired.

Schumpeter played with the idea of leaving Harvard and put out feelers. Yale presented him with an attractive offer, but to his surprise both colleagues and students begged not to go. Overwhelmed by the sympathy he encountered he turned the offer down. At this time he was working on *Capitalism, Socialism and Democracy* (1942). The core of the work turns on a theory of democracy and the application of an economic framework to the analysis of the political process. The conception of political parties as vote-maximising institutions founded a new branch of the social sciences: the so-called 'New Political economy' or 'Economic Theory of Politics', Anthony Downs being a leading representative of this new tendency. Apart from this, the book was conceived as a supplement to *Business Cycles* and was intended to extend the analysis of the total process of capitalist development – a seamless whole – by taking account of political and institutional aspects. The book became his greatest success, even though it was immediately attacked from quite various directions. It was the book of a conservative or, as Samuelson said, of a 'reactionary'. The book was translated into sixteen languages and remains up to the present day required reading for several disciplines. Schumpeter responds to his question 'can capitalism continue?' affirmatively from the purely economic perspective; not least with the claim that 'trustified capitalism' was capable of a higher rate of innovation than was possible under normal competitive conditions. The monopolistic practices of large firms, especially the institutionalisation of research and development, do not in his view portend a reduction in the rate of technical change. Capitalism would not break on the decline in the rate of profit, as Marx had predicted. The danger came from another direction: from the disappearance of the entrepreneurial function, from the erosion of social strata that protected capitalism, from the anticapitalist orientation of intellectuals as well as from the gradual destruction of the institutional framework of capitalist society. The road to socialism was for these reasons inescapable (see especially Schumpeter 1950: 456; and Aham 1984).

During the war years Schumpeter's condition deteriorated markedly. He suffered from reports from the slaughter of entire peoples in Europe, and the allied bombing. His rituals in respect to Johanna,

{36}------------------------------------------------

his second wife, and his mother assumed ever more bizarre forms. In his diary and also in public he began to make a habit of expressing racist sentiments. For a third time he took sharply against Keynes and his economics. His third wife slipped increasingly into the role of carer.

But Schumpeter's capacity for work remained unbroken and his reputation great. In 1948 he became President of the American Economic Association. He devoted all his available effort to a final great work which he was destined to leave uncompleted: the monumental *History of Economic Analysis*.<sup>19</sup> He died on 8 January 1950 of a stroke. The text, almost 1300 pages, was edited and published by his wife, who was helped by Richard Goodwin, one of Schumpeter's former students (Schumpeter 1954a). It proved to be an invaluable source for anyone interested in the history of theory, and will remain a reference work of lasting importance.

Now we turn to consider more closely some selected writings of Schumpeter. Taking the work as a whole, we gain the impression of a scholar searching for a 'universal social science'. This is what he said about the work of Adam Smith, but there are reasons to believe that he wished to develop a modern version of Smith's achievement.

### 3.3 The search for a 'universal social science'

#### 3.3.1 Das Wesen und der Hauptinhalt der theoretischen Nationalökonomie

Schumpeter made a prominent debut in 1908 with *Das Wesen und der Hauptinhalt der theoretischen Nationalökonomie*, a book that exceeds 650 pages. It quickly established the 25-year-old's reputation as a rising star of German-language economics. Some of the arguments contained in the book had already seen the light of day, two years earlier in the essay 'Über die mathematische Methode der theoretischen Ökonomie [On the Mathematical Method of Theoretical Economics]' (1906). Here he invoked Jevons and Walras in maintaining that economics should model itself on the so-called exact sciences and make use of mathematics in the study and representation of general economic interdependence: since the concepts of political economy are 'quantitative', he argued, 'then our discipline is mathematical in character. Its judgements have the character of equations' (1952 [1906]: 534). Remarkably, in *Wesen* he failed to adhere to his own precept, and this made the book much longer than it would otherwise have been.

The 'Foreword' precociously adopts the pose of a mature scholar who, in possession of extensive knowledge and unerring judgement, sees clearly how things are going to turn out. He confides to the reader that 'I view with composure the new scientific era which, if I am not mistaken, is already beginning to dawn' (1908: XXII). He seeks himself to map out the path to be taken by this still young science. Even in his early years, this mastermind sketched out a grand plan whose aim was the revolutionising of the discipline, no less. But to do so he needed to first place the weaknesses of established theory under the spotlight. Only then can he take up his position as the pioneer. Schumpeter wants to be the greatest economists of his era.

It was during his time as a student in Vienna that he first developed an interest in mathematical economics; at first, influenced by Jevons' *Theory of Political Economy*, he adopted the view that economics could, with the aid of mathematics, become an exact science like physics or chemistry. *Das Wesen* is devoted to this idea. The book is directed primarily to a German readership (1908: XXI) which, thanks to the predominance of the Younger Historical School, led by the influential Gustav Schmoller, has lost any sense of the power of 'pure' theory. Schumpeter was reacting in his own way to the *Methodenstreit* that had raged during the 1880s between Carl Menger and Schmoller. Menger had condemned the one-sidedness of historism, but Schumpeter adopted a more nuanced position, arguing that while pure theory was indispensable, so was the application of a historical method – each had a relevance when properly applied. Methodological pluralism was vital in a discipline like economics. As regards pure theory, the sole acceptable position was that of 'methodological individualism' – one of Schumpeter's many successful neologisms (1908, Part I, Ch.

{37}------------------------------------------------

VI).<sup>20</sup> By going back to the decisions of individual economic subjects a number of insights can be deduced that are taken to be valid as general laws. In an instrumentalist sounding passage, whose sense would be radicalised by Milton Friedman almost fifty years later, Schumpeter emphasised: 'It is not a matter of how these things do actually relate, but rather how we have to schematise or stylise them so that we might as much as possible further our aims; hence, which conception, based on the results of pure economics, is the most practical' (1908: 93-4; for a similar formulation 1906: 531-2). He added: 'For the economist the nature of the economy is a matter of indifference . . . We need to keep our attention on that which we wish to achieve – in this case the phenomenon of price – and refer only to that which is absolutely necessary to the achievement of our aim' (1908: 94, emphasis in original).

The view that the assumptions of theory need have no connection with the world that is to be explained has been repeatedly, and rightly, criticised (e.g. Rieter 1985: 43; Schneider 2001: 448). Nor is Schumpeter's conception that the goal to which theory is directed serves as a yardstick of any use. Contra Schumpeter, it is in no respect evident that Walrasian theory is especially frugal with regard to given data or independent variables. If the aim is to determine the relative prices and the distribution of income of a given system, we do not need information on all the individuals populating this system and their preferences – a level of information that the empirical researcher will never in any case command. Instead, for this purpose details of the level of total production, technical alternatives and wages paid are sufficient, as implied by the Classical economists (see Kurz 2006). Schumpeter's idea that the whole is equal to the sum of its parts is difficult to sustain in a world where the phenomenon of externalities for purposively-directed action – a central theme of Schumpeterian design – is ubiquitous. However, as we shall see below, Schumpeter was to adhere to this methodological stance even when turning to an explanation of economic development.

In *Wesen* Schumpeter proposed 'a kind of Monroe Doctrine' (1908: 536). Economic theory has nothing to do with metaphysics and is to be strictly distinguished from other social sciences as well as from philosophy. In particular, it is entirely independent of psychology and biology. Any call for a psychological foundation to the concept of utility involves a serious misunderstanding. The concept of utility is no more than supposition on the part of theoreticians, a mere assumption: 'It is economic facts, not psychological states, that prompt us to put forward the concept' (1908: 542). Schumpeter insists 'that there is no connection between economics and psychology, neither methodological nor material, to which we need have resort for our results' (1908: 544). The 25-year-old stated quite bluntly that other social scientific disciplines 'have little, or nothing, to offer us. In the interest of clarity we need to emphasise their triviality and discard this ballast' (1908: 553). Schumpeter was explicit in moving economic theory closer to the natural sciences, and away from the social sciences: 'Methodologically and epistemologically pure economics is in essence more a "natural science" and its theorems "natural laws"' (1908: 536).

Schumpeter's apodictic statements must have seemed precocious and provocative to large numbers of his contemporaries. But what at the time appeared of no consequence did in the course of the discipline's development come to be, at least for mainstream neoclassical economics – and in more or less the exact manner that Schumpeter predicted with youthful incaution, but great foresight, in 1908.

Despite the uncoupling of economics from other disciplines and its purely instrumental formation, Schumpeter continued, an economic theory based upon Walras made significant insight into human behaviour possible – it was indeed the first genuine science of human (economic) behaviour. Schumpeter argued aggressively, offensively, for an instrumentalised theory of general equilibrium: 'The supreme interest of pure economics' consisted in the fact 'that it represents an extent of the domain of exact thinking' (1908: 563). This follows from the 'extensive generality' and 'unambiguous determinacy of our system of equilibrium', as well as its close connection with a 'very large amount of facts' (1908: 564).

{38}------------------------------------------------

He went on to argue that the task of economic theory was to analyse the interactions of agents pursuing their own ends on their own account; to investigate the interdependence of 'economic quantities'; and to show via the method of comparative statics – or as Schumpeter called it, the 'method of variation' – how a change in one of the model's parameters brought about a new 'equilibrium'. This equilibrium was itself conceived as a conjectural point to which the entire economic system gravitated following alterations to these parameters. Léon Walras's approach permitted the analysis of general interdependence and was, according to Schumpeter, of indispensable value for an understanding of economics relationships. He emphasised that he was, above all, concerned to make the work of 'the great master of exact theory', a kind of 'Magna Carta' for economics, known to a wider public (1908: 261; also Kurz 1989). Besides Walras, Schumpeter also noted the views of Menger, Böhm-Bawerk, Wieser, Pantaleoni, Irving Fisher and other contemporary writers. He synthesised their work into an extensive system that showed little concern for off-putting details and open questions, but focused upon the task of laying out the general outline, the general tendency of theory. The exact conditions for the existence, uniqueness and stability of equilibrium were not discussed, nor whether the resulting solutions made any sense (that is, involved non-negative prices and quantities). The focus was on the long-run equilibrium of the economy, but there was no real discussion of the conditions required by this long-term perspective, nor any consideration given to the possibility of coherently formulating such a theory.<sup>21</sup>

Interestingly, Schumpeter had already in his early writings put forward the view that the Walrasian static method was incapable of dealing with the prime dynamic element, 'economic novelty' or 'innovation'. The theory implied an agent capable of great adaptation to any given conditions, a 'boring man of equilibrium' (1908: 568); but it took no account of the agents who revolutionised prevailing conditions, the 'entrepreneur'.<sup>22</sup> 'What a terrible figure is cut by the economic subject timorously seeking an equilibrium, without ambition, without entrepreneurial spirit, in short, without lust and life! And where are all the wishes and actions that raise everyday life out of the dust?' (1908: 567). He insists that: 'So far, only statics has been reasonably well-investigated, and in this book we direct ourselves for the most part to this. Dynamics has barely been studied, it is a "land of the future"' (1908: 183). He leaves a survey of this country for his next book, and closes this one with the remark that Walrasian theory, despite its obvious limitations, has brought to light a 'small group of assured truths'; it is, according to Schumpeter, a 'light within a sea of darkness' (1908: 626).

Schumpeter's gushing praise for Walras's work must have gone down badly with his teachers, and their mentor Carl Menger. Neither would they have taken any pleasure from the news that the future of the discipline lay in mathematics. Both Menger and Böhm-Bawerk had repeatedly criticised the project of general equilibrium which Walras had embraced, and regarded the use of mathematics at best with scepticism. The use of simultaneous equations ran contrary to the 'causal genetics' of the Austrians; for Böhm-Bawerk such usage was simply a 'deadly sin against all scientific logic'.

Before I continue, I should briefly comment upon the existence and properties of a solution to the Walrasian system. As is well known, for the most part Walras had gone no further than count the number of equations and the number of unknowns, and was convinced that where they were equal the developed model would automatically generate an economically meaningful solution. This would provide a representation in abstract form of the relations prevailing in a real economy. But this counting method is no substitute for a proof of existence, and even if a solution does exist, there is no reason per se that it should be economically meaningful. In particular, Schumpeter did not see that an equilibrium can only be formulated with the aid of inequalities, and that there are no grounds for the assumption that for every initial endowment of productive factors all markets for productive services can be cleared. He also did not understand that for any given endowment of concrete capital goods it is not possible to generally define a single rate of return. In the first three

{39}------------------------------------------------

editions of the *Éléments* Walras had adopted this view, and only abandoned it in the fourth (see Kurz and Salvadori 1997: Chs. 1 and 14). Hence his theory turned out to be limited to the short run and not, as he had originally thought, the long run.

#### 3.3.2 Theorie der wirtschaftlichen Entwicklung

In 1911 the *Theorie der wirtschaftlichen Entwicklung* was published, nearly six hundred pages long. A substantially revised second edition followed in 1926; then translations into Italian (1932), English (1934), French (1935), Japanese (1935) and Spanish (1944). The book 'should to a great extent fulfil promises that I have occasionally made [in *Wesen und Hauptinhalt*] regarding mainly critical elaborations' (1912: VII).<sup>23</sup> Schumpeter had just turned 28. The underlying vision evident from his very earliest writings was beginning to become clearer. He would devote the greater part of his working to the impact and explanation of the phenomenon of economic development. But he does not want to seem especially hard-working; he seeks regard as especially original. In his daily life, or while socialising, he doggedly cultivates the image of a genius to whom things come easily; at night he works long and hard preparing the next stroke of genius. His overweening ambition spurred him on, and within a few years he had produced an oeuvre of considerable measure.

Schumpeter begins *Theorie* with an investigation of the 'circular flow of the economy'. More precisely, he assumes a stationary economy in which, day in, day out, the same processes of production and consumption occur. The behaviour of individual agents is based on routines, which for them derive from experience. The economy is ordered to a 'value system' which replicates the 'geology of this mountain of experience'. This value system can be analysed with the aid of static equilibrium theory. In this way Schumpeter shapes Walras's theory into one relating to the case of stationary economies.<sup>24</sup> In such economies, according to Schumpeter, there are no profits, only wages and salaries and rents paid on scarce land. It is quite plain to him that in making this claim he goes against all hitherto existing theory – whether Classical, Marxist, marginalist or Austrian. He concedes that this view seems to be a 'crass contradiction of the facts' (1912: 48): but all the same it was the only correct view.

The model of a stationary economy can already be found in François Quesnay's *Tableau économique*, in Marx's 'schemes of simple reproduction', and in Marshall's *Principles* – all works with which Schumpeter was familiar. Even the notion that in special circumstances in a stationary economy only wages and rents are paid has its precursors: Quesnay, for example, or Marx's concept of 'simple commodity production'. But stationarity is not necessarily synonymous with the absence of profit and interest, as Schumpeter contends. Not only Classical theory, but also marginalist theory, concludes that the rate of profit in a stationary economy is in general positive. According to the latter, only in an economy saturated in capital does it tend to zero; and this happens because the social rate of time preference is equal to zero (Samuelson 1943, 1982).

Whoever seeks to take the guild on in this way has vaulting ambition. Schumpeter is consequently prompted to review some of the existing explanations of profit, so that he can show them to be untenable. Especially interesting is his rejection of Böhm-Bawerk's attempt to derive interest from, among other things, an innate human preference for present as against future needs. Borrowing from Wieser, Schumpeter emphasises that in a circular flow there can be no 'systematic underestimation of means of production as against products' (1912: 52). Positive time preference is the consequence of a positive rate of interest, and not its cause.<sup>25</sup> Schumpeter goes a step further in saying that in the economy under consideration there is no money rate of interest, since no capital is accumulating, and there is consequently no need of credit. All incomes are consumed either as wages or rent (1912: 67). There are neither 'entrepreneurs' nor 'capitalists', but only 'static enterprise managers' who draw a salary for their work. 'There is simply no basis for interest,' insists Schumpeter (1912: 91). He seems to have overlooked that, even if producer credit is negligible in the stationary system, there is nonetheless consumer credit between the young and the old, between those members of society who find themselves in difficulty and others, and so on.

{40}------------------------------------------------

The economy in question, he goes on, is 'calm, passive, determined by circumstance, stationary, static'. There is no sign of anything like 'the possibility of development from within' (my emphasis). Likewise, there are here no 'crises' (1912: 91). Schumpeter sees Walras as the prime representative of the static perspective, but likewise the Austrians, he continues, 'naturally depict no more than the circular flow of the economy' (1912: 100). This is an assessment that did not please his teachers. In short: all previous theory is static, what is needed is a dynamic theory.

He did allow that Classical economics had made some tentative steps in this direction. Yet the 'sole major attempt in respect of the problem of development' is said to have come from Karl Marx:

Only Marx . . . has sought to deal with the development of economic life using the medium of economic theory. His theories of accumulation, impoverishment and economic breakdown are based upon purely economic reasoning; and he keeps unwaveringly in view the development of economic life as such, not seeking only to penetrate its circular flow at one particular point in time. But the bases of his theory are nevertheless purely static in character – they are after all the bases of Classical economists.

(Schumpeter 1912: 98)

Schumpeter leaves it at this. Nothing at all is said here of what Marx's exact merits might be, nor any indication given to the reader of where Schumpeter's own approach differed from that of Marx. As we know, in Marx it is the 'coercive law of competition' that forces the permanent revolution of the system of production: producers have to constantly develop and introduce new products and methods of production if they are to maintain their place in existing markets or evade the competition by escaping to entirely new markets. Competition means rivalry, and only he survives who successfully innovates or imitates.<sup>26</sup> Innovation is not the outcome of any special propensity on the part of a group of humans characterised by extraordinary capabilities, but the result of a behaviour of the owners of capital and their representatives which is forced upon them by the market. Extraordinary abilities are usually sufficient advantage to remain in a market, but they are not the driving force of development, merely its medium. The impulse originates in the structure of the capitalist economy and is transformed into the behaviour of agents: capitalists are condemned to innovate or go out of business by their interaction with their competitors, their 'inimical brothers' (Marx). Without too much of an exaggeration it could be said that capitalists breed capitalism as much as capitalism breeds capitalists. (See also [Chapter 4](#).)

Schumpeter's perspective on this is quite different. In Marx, the whole determines the behaviour of the parts; Schumpeter, by contrast, seeks to maintain his adherence to methodological individualism, where the parts determine the whole. Which parts? Even for Schumpeter it is a matter of that kind of development 'that the economy autonomously generates.' He insists that this form of endogenous development is 'most fundamentally a phenomenon that requires an economic explanation' (1912: 103) But unlike Marx, Schumpeter does not invoke a systemic foundation to motivate his explanation, but instead a 'second type of economic action': the 'creative action' in the domain of the economy. The 'new agents' (1912: 105) is according to Schumpeter the linchpin of the explanation he is looking for.

Not everyone is capable of creative endeavour, not everyone has the willpower to leave the familiar and try something new. Apart from the 'rational' type there is 'a special real human group', men with leadership qualities, an 'elite'. The first type is 'static' and 'hedonistic'; the second 'dynamic' and 'energetic'. The first seeks to fit in with given conditions as best as possible, while the second seeks to overthrow given conditions and create new ones to suit himself or herself. The two types are quite different in the motivation of their actions as well as the energy put into them. The second type 'adds something to what existed', 'alters economic organisation', 'thrusts his goods upon the market', creates 'artificial' needs and demand. He is quite 'indifferent' to the pleasure which is the sole aim of the hedonist. In explaining his behaviour one needs to cite 'semi-pathological elements'.

{41}------------------------------------------------

The dynamic type is a 'real lad', no 'moaning Minnie' who constantly and anxiously asks whether the payoff in pleasure for any and every effort is worth it (1912: 137). In a word:

Such men get on with things, because they are incapable of not so doing. Their actions are the most wonderful, brilliant moment that economic life offers the observer, and any static and hedonistic explanation pales besides them.

(Schumpeter 1912: 138)

The dynamic person takes pleasure in his 'powerful social position' and in his own activity. The way forward is an end in itself. He takes 'pleasure is success', in his 'triumphs over others'. For him there is no equilibrium, only the restless search for new fields of activity. He wants to found business empires and economic dynasties, he wants to subordinate others to his will, make them subjects, he wants to go down in history as a great ruler, as a power to be reckoned with, as of old did important kings and generals.<sup>27</sup>

Before we go on, it might be critically noted that it is unclear quite how the postulate of a 'dynamic' human type, dedicated to the overthrow of prevailing conditions and repeatedly succeeding in so doing, can ever be converted into an explanation of endogenous economic development. If one supposes that the cause of dynamism is exogenous, only the course taken by the system once set in motion is endogenous. Many authors have drawn attention to this contradiction, among them Kuznets (1940) and Rieter (1985).

The circular flow is broken by 'entrepreneurs' who impose 'new combinations' (1912: 170). An entrepreneur is 'a chief specialising in the economic domain' (1912: 173). 'New combinations' are, according to Schumpeter: (1) product innovations; (2) process innovations; (3) the opening of new markets; (4) the opening up, or conquest, of new sources of raw materials and semi-finished goods; (5) the 'implementation of a reorganisation, such as the creation of a monopoly position, or the breaking of a monopoly'. Innovations are the 'supreme fact in the economic history of capitalist society'. Technological and organisational change ceaselessly revolutionises the entire economic system. It brings into being new goods and lines of work, and weeds out old ones. It forces through far-reaching social changes. It involves 'creative destruction', as Schumpeter later called it, since it would be naïve to assume that it produces winners but no losers. The losers are firstly the direct competitors of innovators, then their employees, then their suppliers, etc.

The establishment of new combinations requires that resources be taken from old combinations and put at the disposal of the new. This occurs via the process of credit creation. He who innovates needs purchasing power. A new entrepreneur is usually not in a position to make use of profits accumulated from the past (1912: 192). The banking system is called upon to perform the important function of meeting his need for credit: 'One could say without being far wrong that the banker creates money' (1912: 197). He adds: 'if the entrepreneur is king of the market, then the banker is the ephor, the overseer' (1912: 198). More: the entrepreneur 'can only become an entrepreneur by first becoming a debtor' (1912: 208). In principle, all economic development has need of credit (1912: 212). This purchasing power is added to that which already exists. Assuming that resources in the stationary cycle are fully employed, the increased monetary demand leads to a rise in the price level, and thence to the redirection of goods towards the innovator; the credit-induced inflation operates like a tax on 'static economic agents' and leads to a redistribution of productive resources. Although interest is primarily a monetary phenomenon, in the final instance it rests upon a real economic factor: the increase in productivity brought about by innovation.

Schumpeter's idea of 'new' combinations was not so new after all. It can be found in many earlier writings, including those of German authors (see Streissler 1994). Especially noteworthy are the contributions of Albert Schäffle. In 1868 he was given a chair in the University of Vienna and during the few years that he stayed there before returning to Germany he was an inspiration to numerous Austrian economists (see Hennings 1997). This influence extended also to Schumpeter (Borchardt

{42}------------------------------------------------

1961; Balabkins 2003). Schäffle not only anticipated Schumpeter's conception of technical, organisational and commercial innovation, he gave the figure of the 'entrepreneur' a key role in the developmental process, as had other German writers before him. Moreover, he went further and wrote of crowds of imitators, which in Schumpeter came to play an important role in connection with the propagation of an innovative impulse.<sup>28</sup>

A comparison with the classical distinction between 'normal' profit from 'surplus' or 'extra' profit is needed here (see also [Chapter 2](#)). According to the classical economists, under competitive conditions the economic system tended to a situation in which the value of the capital advanced yields a uniform rate of profit. Innovations displace this process of gravitation to a new gravitational point. The successful innovator enjoys for a more or less lengthy period a surplus above and beyond the normal rate of profit, what Ricardo called 'surplus profits'. Schumpeter knows only the latter, the extra profit arising from innovation, which then gets gradually eroded as a consequence of the diffusion and generalisation of an innovation in the economic system, and the imitative behaviour of competitors. The core component of Schumpeter's story could have been entirely accounted for in the analytical apparatus of classical economics. And he would have had no basis for the assumption that in the new long run position of the economic system – the new 'circular flow' – the normal rate of profit would equal zero.

Schumpeter explained the emergence and erosion of surplus profit as follows. Typically, economic development does not follow a steady and constant path, for innovations 'come in droves' and are 'linked together'. Industrial transformation is never 'harmonious advance' but involves 'random shocks to the state of equilibrium' resulting in the destruction of some existing firms and lines of production. Whoever made a fundamental innovation made it easier, or even necessitated, the emergence of other entrepreneurs: those that linked it to their own, improving innovation, and those who simply imitated so that they might not be ejected from the market. The absorption and assimilation of shocks involves 'a process of the adoption of the new and the adaptation of the economy to the new'. Once the force of an innovative impulse dissipated a crisis occurred. This process led necessarily to a depression, defined by Schumpeter as 'the struggle of an economy for a new state of equilibrium'. Schumpeter thought that the upswing contained the germ of its termination and eventually resulted in a new stationary state.

There are various ideas about the origin of Schumpeter's thesis that in this stationary state profit and interest were absent. In my view he took the idea from Walras. In his *Éléments*, Walras turned to the case of a stationary economy once he had dealt with the problem of isolated exchange; here consumption goods were produced, but supposedly no new capital goods. According to Walras (1954 [1874]: 269), there were also no prices for the latter, only prices for the services of existing capital goods. Since the 'rate of net income', Walras' expression for the rate of interest, is itself the relation between the net price of the services provided by a capital good and the price of a new item of the capital good, there could therefore be no interest in a stationary economy. Interest could only arise in a 'progressing' economy in which capital goods are produced – this is dealt with in Part V of *Éléments*, which significantly deals with 'Capital Accumulation and Credit'.

Walras's faulty logic is obvious: just because in a stationary economy there is no net accumulation of capital, this does not mean that durable capital goods are not produced and reproduced. Walras himself implies that the use of capital goods leads to their depreciation and that they have to be replaced. Moreover, the net prices of the services of capital goods can only be defined if depreciation is deducted from the gross price. According to Walras, this latter element is proportional to the price of the capital good. In turn, this means that one cannot know the price of the service without knowing the price of the capital good, and vice versa. Schumpeter, it seems, merely repeats Walras' mistake. So for instance he puts forward the view, reminiscent of Walras, that the stationarity of the economy involved the presence only of circulating capital goods (1912: 64, 67, 69).

{43}------------------------------------------------

Simon Kuznets (1940) advanced the criticism of Schumpeter's theory of the long wave that there was not one innovation capable of engendering such great fluctuations in investment behaviour. To generate long waves, as Christopher Freeman emphasised (1982), there had to be the diffusion of entire clusters of related innovations – so-called 'new technological systems'. Nathan Rosenberg (2000) has remarked that innovations are much less discontinuous than Schumpeter assumed. And finally – Schumpeter was certainly able to explain an investment boom through the diffusion of new technologies and the associated band-wagon effect, but he had no satisfactory explanation of depression and the lower turning point of the cycle. In fact, he called a deep depression 'pathological'.

#### 3.3.3 Epochen der Dogmen- und Methodengeschichte

Max Weber's invitation to the barely 30-year-old Schumpeter to contribute an essay on the history of economics entitled 'Epochen der Dogmen- und Methodengeschichte [Epochs of the History of Economic Analysis and Methods]' to the *Grundriss der Sozialökonomik* [Compendium of Socio-Economics] (published in 1914) expressed the considerable respect in which the new Graz appointee was held by the guild of German-speaking social scientists. The *Grundriss* was no more and no less than an assessment of the current state of research in the various fields of the discipline and may perhaps be compared to *The New Palgrave* in Great Britain.<sup>[29](#)</sup>

The 1914 essay of around 100 pages formed the basis for his later *History of Economic Analysis* (1954a), and continued a renowned Austrian tradition: demonstration of an interest in the history and development of the discipline. But how can someone who has just turned thirty review and comment upon such a range of material? Naturally he cannot actually do so, but there was probably no-one of Schumpeter's age who could have done a better job, and only a few older than him who might have been his equal in the task. Schumpeter's essay is impressive, even taking account of the fact that it draws heavily on two sources: Böhm-Bawerk's *Geschichte und Kritik der Kapitalzinsteorien* [History and Critique of Theories of Capital and Interest], and Karl Kautsky's edition of Marx's *Theorien über den Mehrwert* [Theories of Surplus Value]. He begins with a discussion of the 'Development of Social Economy into a Science'. This ranges from antiquity via the scholastics to mercantilist writing. Then he turns to the 'Discovery of the Economic Circular Flow.' Here Schumpeter is entirely influenced by Marx. For example, he says of William Petty that he 'had sought to penetrate and interpret theoretically the material . . . in a way hardly ever repeated since' (1914a: 32). Marx had of course regarded Petty as the real founder of political economy. Without any reference to Marx, Schumpeter employs the concept of 'vulgar economics' to some mercantilist authors (1914a: 35). Like Marx, he characterises Quesnay as 'one of the greatest and most original thinkers in our field' (1914a: 40) and substantiates this in words that remind one of Marx: with the development of the concept of circular flow in the *Tableau Économique*, Quesnay and the Physiocrats succeeded in grasping the entire 'process of economic life', shedding light 'into the interior of the social stream of goods and the process of its constant self-renewal'.

Surprisingly, he deals in this section not only with Turgot but also Adam Smith, of whom he writes:

He was a man whose work was far-ranging and presented with balance, not a man of major new ideas. . . . Treading familiar paths, using the material which lay to hand, this incisive mind created an extraordinary life's work.

(Schumpeter 1914a: 51)

A few lines later he states: 'Today we cannot delude ourselves over Smith's intellectual dimension, for we only too clearly distinguish pedestal from figure' (ibid., 52).

So, no marble statue for the Scottish – and from Schumpeter! What a good thing that one had already been donated in distant Edinburgh. Schumpeter's view concerning Smith's lack of originality still today finds the occasional partisan. But such a conception is ungrounded (see, e.g.,

{44}------------------------------------------------

Streissler 1999). Schumpeter is right to argue that Smith was a formidable hunter and gatherer, always looking for something useable – just like Schumpeter. Both authors were to a large extent concerned with 'new combinations' of received ideas, which, using Schumpeter's concept, involved innovation. Smith was at the same time a synthesiser and systematiser of the first order and furnished several areas with new ideas and insights.

Following this section Schumpeter deals with the 'Classical System and its Impact'. It is the longest of four parts and takes up almost half the entire text. Here too the influence of Marx's *Theorien über den Mehrwert* can be detected on basically each and every page. Disagreeing with the current English interpretation of Alfred Marshall, for whom the Classical economists were more or less crude forerunners of the (marginalist) theory of supply and demand, Schumpeter insisted that their approach was independent and irreducible to later theory – Classical theory and that of Marshall 'were only loosely connected' (55). The 'culminating point' of Classicism was David Ricardo's *Principles* – Ricardo, 'the most important successor' to Smith (53–4) and compared to Smith the more important economist because of the latter's 'relative superficiality' (58, fn. 2). We can read much the same in Marx. Schumpeter makes use of the opportunity to have another go at Smith:

There is a very respectable type of scholar who is rather like that sort of general whose sole concern, and greatest fame lay in the fact of never having been defeated. But the best do not belong to this type.

(Ibid.)

Our Mastermind does not yet know of Keynes and his successes which were to make life difficult for Schumpeter.

Schumpeter accuses Ricardo of analytical narrowness and by contrast praises Marx 'who wanted to understand the life and growth of the social body', and, like Smith, worked at the development of a 'universal social science' (60). He is entirely fascinated by Marxian theory. A footnote stretching over two and a half pages extols Marx's work as 'unique'; beneath the polemical form there lay 'thorough scholarly work' (81 fn.).

[Marx] not only had originality, he also had scholarly talent of the first order. At the time his first volume [of *Das Kapital* in 1867] was published there was no-one who could have measured up to him, neither in power nor in theoretical knowledge.

(81 fn.)

Consequently Marx had also become 'the teacher of many non-socialists', including Schumpeter himself. He also defended Marx from the interpretation that held the labour theory of value for an ethical norm. Without naming him directly, he rebutted Böhm-Bawerk's critique of the third volume of *Capital*, published posthumously in 1895 edited by Engels. Böhm-Bawerk's claim that there was a 'contradiction' between the labour theory of the first volume and the theory of production prices in the third was rejected – 'neither subjectively nor objectively' did grounds exist 'for such a view' (82).

Schumpeter deals in conclusion with the Historical School and marginal utility theory. As in *Wesen*, his judgement on historicism is a balanced one. He credits its focus upon factual material with impelling into economics the concept of evolution, drawing attention to the dynamic elements of economic action. The 'mathematical economics' (of marginalist cast) which he had so highly praised before is now given very little space. In *Wesen* he had treated this work as the white hope of economics; now he displays a remarkable degree of reserve. Its domain of application is said to be

... a limited one, and its results go only a little further than a more accurate and sharper presentation, so that its general utility is a matter for discussion – and one might question whether

{45}------------------------------------------------

at the present time learning such a special instrumentarium is worthwhile for an economist concerned mostly with the acquisition of the results of theory (110).<sup>30</sup>

The reader might well rub his eyes in astonishment. How does Schumpeter think that the 'results' of theory can be acquired and understood without ascertaining the rectitude of the manner in which they were arrived at? And can one understand such results at all without understanding their derivation, the assumptions upon which they are based as well as their interrelation? Which particular results of theory were scientifically secure in 1914? And how does the theory in question relate to Classical theory, to which Schumpeter had ascribed an independent character?

In the closing pages Schumpeter makes the surprising claim, among others, that Alfred Marshall 'had in particular taken over Walras's entire teaching' (116). He sees the difference between the Classics and marginal utility theory as follows. While the former dealt with the 'great objective facts and tendencies', marginal utility theory investigated the 'basic facts of economic action' (117). The second important difference lay in the 'renunciation of the quantity of labour as regulator and measure of commodity values – together with the presentation and implementation of the perspective of use value' in marginal utility theory (118). With regard to this characterisation we can note the following apparent paradox. Many, including Schumpeter, overlook the fact that leading marginal utility theorists – from Jevons through Böhm-Bawerk, and from John Bates Clark to Philip Wicksteed – were of the view that in equilibrium the relative prices of commodities were equal to the relative quantities of labour employed in their production. Ricardo (and Marx) only admitted the possibility of this in very limited cases. While Ricardo, a stock jobber, was well aware of the phenomenon of compound interest as well as its implications for value theory, the early representatives of marginalism ignored this phenomenon, with fatal results for their theory of capital.

### 3.4 A life's work and its impact

In the foregoing I have discussed incidentally the reception of some of Schumpeter's work. In concluding, I would like to sketch out some elements on the impact that this work had. This cannot of course be comprehensive in any respect; a few observations must suffice.

#### 3.4.1 Hayek's theory of the business cycle

Other Austrian economists shared Schumpeter's interest in the business cycle, in particular Ludwig von Mises, and also Friedrich August von Hayek, who built on the work of Mises (Kurz 2000a, 2000b). Methodologically Hayek (1931) followed Schumpeter very closely. He too started from an economy in a stable condition of general equilibrium, a stationary state, and thought of the cycle as a transition set off by a disturbance that led to a new stationary equilibrium. However, he did part company with Schumpeter in important respects, first of all with regard to the character of the disturbance in question. Hayek discussed only two types of cause. The first involved an autonomous change of agents' preferences – an increase or decrease of the rate of time preference, and hence a fall or rise of gross saving. The change in 'voluntary saving', according to Hayek, brought about a more or less smooth transition to a new long-term equilibrium. The second type involved a change in the choice of the cost-minimising technique on the part of the producer induced by monetary policy. If the banking system sets the money rate of interest below that of the equilibrium rate of interest (as conceived by marginal productivity theory) this provides an incentive for producers to embark on longer, more 'roundabout' processes of production. An extension of producers' credit emerges and, as in Schumpeter, there is a rise in the price of productive factors, attracted in this way to stages of temporally-staggered production further away from the production of consumption goods. Assuming that nothing had altered in the preferences of agents, the reduction in the output of consumer goods – 'forced saving' – eventually is bound to lead to a rise in the prices of consumer goods, and hence to an increase in profitability in those stages of production

{46}------------------------------------------------

that are closer to final output, i.e. consumption goods. This however reveals that the original lengthening of the production process was a mistake and has led to a misallocation of resources.

Hayek's explanation applied to the normal business cycle, but not to the longrun trend. While Schumpeter ascribed a central role to the banking system in promoting innovative impulses, Hayek saw in the discretionary power of the banking system the root of all evil: its decisions on interest rate policy generated costly ups and downs that, supposedly, could not occur in an economy based upon exchange in kind. Schumpeter did not think highly of Hayek's theory and said as much in private correspondence with Piero Sraffa, Hayek's sharpest critic.

#### 3.4.2 Long waves in theory and in practice

Although the idea that there are long waves of economic activity had been stated before Schumpeter, modern interest in them began with Schumpeter more than anyone. Attempts have been made to demonstrate the existence of such long waves using spectral analytic procedures, and the global stagnation and stagflation of the early 1970s fed conjecture that this was the final phase of the fourth Kondratiev wave. The microelectronic revolution is hailed as the key technology in the fifth Kondratiev. Instead of business cycles one now occasionally talks of growth cycles. There is an extensive literature on this; see especially the work of Freeman (1982, 1983) and van Duijn (1983).

#### 3.4.3 Evolutionary economics

In recent years there has developed a new line of research, drawing on Schumpeter among others, and which has promoted 'evolutionary economics'. This has grown in importance and has since become established in the form of scientific societies and specialist journals. It is open to question whether the invocation of Schumpeter is always appropriate.

The evolutionary approach in economics is especially associated with the publication of Nelson and Winter's *Evolutionary Theory of Economic Change* (1982). Stanley Metcalfe's 1995 Schumpeter Lectures in Graz modelled the process of creative destruction as an evolutionary process (Metcalfe 1998). He did this by using the laws of transition in populations that R.A. Fisher introduced in his book *The Genetical Theory of Natural Selection* (1930).

#### 3.4.4 A Schumpeterian model of endogenous growth

Finally we can draw the attention to an important branch of 'new' or 'endogenous' growth theory. In the second half of the 1980s emphasis was laid upon the positive external effects of capital accumulation – especially that of human capital and knowledge – that worked against the tendency of the profit rate to decline. From the early 1990s, however, negative externalities were introduced within the framework of a so-called 'Schumpeterian model' – here the book by Aghion and Howitt was important (1998). In this model Schumpeter's 'heroic entrepreneur' is only a marginal figure. The focus is rather on the work done in R&D within firms and its realisation in the form of marketable new products and instruments of production. There are winners and losers in the process of innovation, and the question arises as to what the optimal social rate of innovation might be.

We may conclude by saying that the power of attraction of our mastermind's ideas is in no respect exhausted.
