## 5 On the growth of knowledge about the role of knowledge in economic growth

A critical assessment of recent literature on growth theory<sup>1</sup>

The empirical element in economic theory – the only part which is concerned, not merely with implications but with causes and effects, and which leads therefore to conclusions which, at any

{70}------------------------------------------------

rate in principle, are capable of verification – consists of propositions about the acquisition of knowledge.

(Hayek 1937: 33)

### 5.1 Introduction

After Paul Romer had discovered that his earlier formalisations of growth theory were incompatible with the assumption of the non-rivalry of a good, what he called 'research', he wrote that this blunder

may seem a trifling matter in an area of theory that depends on so many other short cuts. After all, if one is going to do violence to the complexity of economic activity by assuming that there is an aggregate production function, how much more harm can it do to be sloppy about the difference between rival and nonrival goods?

(Romer 1994: 15–16)

The question is certainly justified, and Romer's answer was perfectly clear: the damage arising is major. But 'sloppiness' already seems to have become endemic in this sub-discipline. As the same Romer stated two years later:

Only 30 years ago many economists still objected to a mathematical statement of the relationship between output and capital in terms of an aggregate production function and an aggregate stock of capital,  $Y = f(K, L)$

(Romer 1996: 202; my emphasis)

Things have changed since then. The long-standing proof that an aggregate production function does not exist in the sense that it is consistently derived from micro-information on production processes is, in Vienna-speak, no longer even ignored by many representatives of the discipline.<sup>2</sup> Today the demand for the 'microfoundation' of economic analysis is often treated as fulfilled when the argument makes use of the artifice of the representative, fully informed and immortal agent. Whosoever raises objections to the conception of the aggregate production function or to that of the representative agent quickly finds himself isolated and treated as a maverick. Objections raised by economists from Frank Hahn to Franklin Fisher on the one hand, and from Pierangelo Garegnani to Ian Steedman on the other, fall upon deaf ears and blind eyes.

Given this situation, what is the role of critics from within the guild today? Whatever they say, it echoes without consequences. They may well be right, but those at whom the criticism is aimed are more or less indifferent to it. We seem to be living through a period marked by a decided degree of self-satisfaction on the part of the mainstream.

Romer's question posed above can be extended by adding a measure of polemic: how much 'sloppiness', from the perspective of representatives of the theory of endogenous growth, can the sub-discipline withstand?

We can construct a preliminary answer here indirectly: by considering selected statements of the relevant writers made in their publications. I here limit myself to a critical review of the treatment of 'knowledge' in the theory of endogenous growth. I take as my point of departure a passage from an earlier essay of mine:

In Romer (1986) attention focuses on the role of a single state variable called 'knowledge' or 'information'. It is assumed that the information contained in inventions and discoveries has the property of being available to anybody to make use of it at the same time. Poor von Hayek! In other words, information is considered essentially a nonrival good. However, it need not be totally

{71}------------------------------------------------

nonexcludable, that is, it can be monopolized at least for a time. . . . Knowledge is assumed to be cardinally measurable and not to depreciate: it is like perennial capital. No comment!

(Kurz 1997: 15)

My closing remark was premature. This chapter is divided into ten sections. Section 5.2 provides a brief overview of different forms of knowledge and introduces some of the concepts current in the epistemological literature, which will prove useful in the subsequent discussion. The following sections review some recent contributions to growth theory that are neoclassical in design. The purpose of this survey is not comprehensiveness, but rather that the examples introduced be representative. Section 5.3 deals with Robert Solow's contribution; Section 5.4 Kenneth Arrow's conception of 'learning by doing'; Section 5.5 the first contribution by Paul Romer to the endogenisation of technical progress; Section 5.6 Robert Lucas on the formation of human capital; Section 5.7 Paul Romer's 1990 paper, in which he sought to bring together different ideas into one model; and Section 5.8 Martin Weitzman's conception of 'recombinant growth'. Section 5.9 deals with the role of population growth for economic growth. There are quite remarkable differences of opinion on this in the recent literature. In this context, the existence of methodological differences between these authors will be considered. Section 5.10 pulls together some closing observations.

### 5.2 Different types of knowledge

According to Kenneth Boulding the concept 'knowledge' is best avoided. He has in mind the 'Image' that a person makes of the world or of a particular object or thing. 'Knowledge has an implication of validity, of truth. What I am talking about is what I believe to be true; my subjective knowledge. It is this Image that largely governs my behavior' (Boulding 1956: 5-6). This new concept relates to the cognitive content of human thought. Here we will continue to sue the concept of 'knowledge' so that we might avoid confusion in the discussion of the literature of growth theory. But in so doing we imply nothing about the truth content of knowledge.

In his *Knowledge and Knowledge Production* – a work originally conceived in eight volumes, but only partially realised – Fritz Machlup pointed out that translation of the English term 'knowledge' into German required three words: wissen, kennen and können; or conversely, retranslating these into English, 'to know what', 'to know about' and 'to know how' (Machlup 1980: xiii). His book for the most part turns on these three variant forms of knowledge. For our purposes the second of the three is of little relevance. This leads us to a distinction made by Scheffler (1965: 14) which Machlup largely adopted (1980: 27-33): between propositional knowledge on the one hand, and procedural knowledge on the other. Both concepts can be rather slippery, but they serve to distinguish 'to know what' from 'to know how'.<sup>3</sup>

Knowledge can be public or private. It needs a bearer – 'without a knower, knowledge is an absurdity' (Boulding 1956: 16). The bearer can be a person, a group of persons, or a thing, like for example a book. But books without persons to read and understand them are as good as unwritten.<sup>4</sup> What persons know and how they employ this knowledge depends on a number of factors, among them their perception, their memory, their consciousness, their reason as well as their capacity for rational reflection and the drawing of conclusions (see Audi 1998). The first concept of knowledge, related to a person, concerns the quantity of all ideas, claims of fact, beliefs held and theories of the most various kinds at the disposal of the person at any one specific time. 'Superstition' and 'belief' are here on the same footing. It is not always easy to discriminate between the different elements, since some of them can be related in sometimes complex ways.<sup>5</sup> Nor is it easy to tell what could or should be counted as knowledge for whom. To borrow from Sigmund Freud – what role is played by the subconscious, and what by the unconscious?

{72}------------------------------------------------

The mind of man is a vast storehouse of forgotten memories and experiences. It is much more than a storehouse, however. It is a genuine image affecting our conduct and behavior in ways that we do not understand with our conscious mind.

(Boulding 1956: 53)

Looking ahead to the literature on growth theory, we will mostly ignore problems of this kind and for the sake of argument assume that a given magnitude, which we shall call the stock of social knowledge existing at a given point in time, has meaning and can be ascertained either in fact or in principle. But how?<sup>6</sup> Can one simply count up the fragments of knowledge held by the individual members of a society? What happens when some of these fragments are contradictory? And so far as we are dealing with 'social' knowledge, that possessed by all members of the society in question, should one not treat the given stock of knowledge as that which is actually shared by all members, rather than its total? How do you deal with the double-counting of fragments of knowledge? (We shall see later that this last has very serious consequences in some contributions to growth theory). A change in the stock of knowledge, whether in respect to an individual or a society, typically means that new elements enter and, correspondingly, some older elements are discarded. Here we encounter the next problem arising from the points made above. The discarded elements, whether they be replaced by others or not, could in principle be considered elements of the stock of knowledge, since to 'know' that something is 'false' or dubious is not in itself any less meaningful for a person's understanding of the world than the 'knowledge' that such a person considers certain. It is not unusual for the latter to be the problem.<sup>7</sup> Since many objects of knowledge are of such complexity that there can be no ultimate certainty in respect of their judgement, the individual remains in respect of these objects *nolens volens* in a condition of permanent uncertainty. This also explains why, for example, certain new interpretations are added to existing ones without however replacing them and, depending on the circumstances, first one and then another interpretation will be employed. (Keynes's comments on the capriciousness of businessmen comes to mind.) It is therefore in no respect obligatory that we treat the concept of knowledge here discussed in a solely positive manner – although this is what generally happens. Even ideas that an individual has explicitly rejected can (should?) be included here.<sup>8</sup>

Let us now turn to procedural knowledge, the question of knowledge about the use of knowledge. How do individuals deal with their propositional knowledge? How do they go about 'knowing how', more exactly: 'knowing how to do things'? Procedural knowledge relates to capacities and skills of an individual, the purposive employment of propositional knowledge in decisions and actions. The related mental, emotional, motor and ultimately chemical and physical processes are quite obviously very complex. Since the use of one's own knowledge in a society characterised by the fragmentation of knowledge associated with the division of labour is frequently possible only together with other people, an important aspect of the procedural knowledge of one person is that person's ability to communicate with other persons, to become involved in social interaction. A significant aim of communication is the identification of useful, productive complementarities in knowledge and, possibly, the creation of a common base of knowledge as the precondition of joint work. Sufficient equality among the actors on the one hand and a requisite diversity on the other as conditions to mutually fruitful cooperation were already emphasised by Adam Smith in his discussion of the division of labour.

The growth of knowledge occurs like a metabolic reaction. We take in information and give information out. Interestingly, this happens without the intervention of any law of conservation, such as we know from thermodynamics. The accumulation of knowledge is not simply the difference between messages received and sent. The stock of knowledge is rather something that conforms to internal principles of organisation and growth in relation to information received. According to Boulding, it involves an organic process: knowledge follows principles of growth and development of a kind similar to those encountered in complex organisations and organisms

{73}------------------------------------------------

(Boulding 1956: 17, 35). A discussion of the growth of knowledge of one person can for example be found in Russell (1948) and more recently in Audi (1998: Part 2).

Consciousness of a growth of knowledge influences the human conception of time. In cultures like that of the ancient Aztecs we find a circular conception. Everything periodically and endlessly repeats itself. Such a culture has no idea of history in our sense, only eternal recurrence, circularity. It is only with the formation of a conception of progress that a new conception of time develops: time appears to be unidirectional or linear, and history becomes a kind of one-way street.<sup>9</sup>

An increase of propositional and procedural knowledge is generally related to an increase in the number of objects created by humans, an extension of the number of human artefacts. Increasing knowledge is among other things embodied in a growing variety of the type and quality of products. Growing heterogeneity and diversity in the world of commodities is a characteristic feature of a capitalist economy which generates, uses and also destroys knowledge, what is also modishly known as a 'knowledge economy'. The incredible increase in the kind and quality of available consumption goods intended to satisfy newly awakened needs, as well as the extraordinary increase in general and specialised kinds of work and means of production are facts that cannot be overlooked. Parallel to this there occurs the destruction of older knowledge of the most various kind, from the knowledge embodied in tried and tested techniques of survival to a dramatically reduced biodiversity. In many important respects this development is Janusfaced.<sup>10</sup>

Taking account of all this, the following question must be asked: what can be gained in knowledge if one seeks to represent the long-term growth and development of economies in terms of a one-good model? Or alternatively: can one hope to capture important features of a society whose knowledge increases (in some versions, even more than exponentially) with the aid of models which specifically disregard the increase in knowledge embodied in economic objects of the most diverse sort? Leading representatives of neoclassical growth theory give an affirmative response to this question, at least implicitly; we shall examine how convincing their view of things actually is.

Let us now consider some of the recent models of economic growth – what they have to say about 'knowledge' and how they formalise their underlying ideas. We limit ourselves here to a review of a small but not entirely unrepresentative sample of neoclassical models insofar as they relate to the issues discussed here. Since all of these models relate to the Solow model – whether in critically differentiating themselves from this model, or explicitly seeking to develop it – we begin with Robert Solow. It hardly needs to be said that only those elements directly related to our topic here will be discussed. A general presentation and evaluation is not here intended; for this, the reader should consult the relevant textbooks on growth theory, such as Barro and Sala-i-Martin (1995), Maußner and Klump (1996), Jones (1998) and Aghion and Howitt (1998); see also the essay by Kurz and Salvadori (1998).

### 5.3 Robert Solow (1956, 1957)

In his 1956 paper, Solow does not really deal with knowledge. If one is to talk of knowledge in this paper then it can only relate to his treatment of technology. The growth of social product in the basic variant of his one-good model is linked to the growth of the stock of capital and the associated working population employed.<sup>11</sup> Only with the extension of the basic model does he introduce variable  $A$ , which is intended to represent a scalar and cardinal measure of the stock of technical knowledge, and assumes without any further explanation that this variable grows at the exogenously given proportional rate  $\lambda$ ,  $\lambda < 0$ . We therefore have

$$A(t) = A(0)e^{\lambda t} \quad (5.1)$$

{74}------------------------------------------------

with  $t$  as the time index. A more exact description of the intended meaning of  $A$  can be found in Solow (1957). Confronted with the discovery that the actual growth of the social product cannot be explained even very roughly by the growth of inputs, variable  $A$  and its hypostasised development over time has perforce to bear the main burden of explanation. It has to make the 'unexplained residual' in the growth of the social product – later called the 'Solow residual' – disappear, and it therefore needs further specification. Solow writes:

The variable  $t$  for time appears in  $F$  [the functional sign in the production function] to allow for technical change. It will be seen that I am using the phrase 'technical change' as a short-hand expression for any kind of shift in the production function. Thus slowdowns, speed-ups, improvements in the education of the labor force, and all sorts of things will appear as 'technical change'.

(Solow 1957: 312; emphasis in the original)<sup>12</sup>

'Technical change' is therefore an all-encompassing concept that is supposed to close the gap. Using the terminology introduced in the previous section, this covers both changes in knowing that and in knowing how to do in a society. The relevant part of the growth of knowledge in a society is implicitly a public good freely accessible ('manna from heaven') and which employs time as a means of transportation and diffusion.

But the introduction of factor  $A$  improves not one jot the given explanation, and this has been pointed out by numerous critics, among them Moses Abramovitz. The discovery of a striking unexplained residual could of course have brought about responses differing to that of Solow. But nonetheless, the widespread practice of employing macroeconomic production functions stands in direct contradiction to the propositional knowledge accumulated in the discipline. Can one therefore really believe that things will turn out any better than it did for the tailors of Laputa in Swift's *Gulliver's Travels*?

There is a considerable spread in the (implicit) responses to this. Besides sharp criticism of Solow's approach from many angles there are now, astonishingly enough, an increasing number of studies which – disillusioned by the empirical performance of the models set up by 'new' growth theory – reach back to the first models (for example Mankiw et al. 1992). There is however no ground for satisfaction on the goodness of empirical fit that is thereby achieved. As Felipe and McCombie (2005) show, the result reflects merely a statistical artefact and is no proof of the solidity of the underlying theory of production.

### 5.4 Kenneth Arrow (1962)

One of the first serious attempts to discuss and formalise the accumulation of procedural knowledge can be found in Kenneth Arrow's paper from 1962. He takes as his point of departure the unsatisfactory state of contemporary neoclassical growth theory, inordinately dominated as it was by Solow's  $A$ , an exogenous variable; which therefore rendered worthless for the discussion of economic policy any approach which embodied this assumption. What was needed was investigation of the acquisition of knowledge, of learning processes. Technical progress and the growth of labour productivity were held to be the outcome of processes which were linked to a great extent with those experiences which flowed from economic activity. This led Arrow to the concept of 'learning by doing'.

He was conscious of the fact that he was addressing only a part of the total ensemble of economically-relevant learning processes, but in his view it was a very important element. As he stated in a later paper: 'Knowledge arises from deliberate seeking, but it also arises from observations incidental on other activities.' And: 'An activity will in general have two valuable consequences: the physical outputs themselves and the change in information about other activities' (Arrow 1969: 30–31). Here only the second source of the growth of knowledge is at issue.

{75}------------------------------------------------

This, and the associated increase in productivity, would occur without there being any identifiable use of resources to this end.<sup>13</sup> Like Solow, therefore, Arrow treats the knowledge incorporated in new capital goods as a public good. Since it is impossible to provide a complete description of the processes under consideration, the question arises of the choice of a proxy-magnitude by means of which the quantitative extent of the experience made can be registered as the foundation of the learning process thereby engendered. After some discussion, Arrow decides in favour of cumulative gross investment as an index of the experience undergone.

Before going any further, it will be worthwhile comparing briefly Arrow's conception with Adam Smith's discussion of the division of labour. As is well known, Smith attributed the division of labour to the impact of three elements that increased productivity:

- 1 gains from specialisation;
- 2 the time saved through avoidance of shifts from one activity to another, as well as the related improved use of costly means of production in manufacture;
- 3 induced innovation, i.e. the invention of machinery that takes over ever more of an increasingly complex labour process, that is, replaces labour with machinery. (Smith WN I.i. 5)

Arrow's conception seeks to characterise the effect of the third of these. Hence technical progress is 'embodied' in new capital goods. Their entry into the economic system via gross investment activity compels those working with them to learn how to use the new capital goods. Arrow is therefore not concerned with Smith's idea that the division of labour gives rise to the invention of new capital goods. This is rather given exogenously, and only the implications of the diffusion of new capital goods is open for debate.

Arrow relies on gross investment because he assumes that liquid funds available for reinvestment flow into the procurement of the latest types of capital goods. The greater the gross investment, the greater the induced learning effect attributable to the diffusion of new capital goods. It is not thirst for knowledge and willingness to learn of the workforce that drives the process onward, but rather their confrontation with new machinery and instruments of labour that they have to learn to master if they are to keep their job. Arrow discusses reactive, not active, learning. The appropriation of knowledge is accordingly a compulsory by-product of the process of accumulation.

Interestingly, Arrow does in his paper consider two points that would later play a major part in so-called 'new' growth theory. The first of these is the possibility of increasing returns to scale in relation to capital and labour. Secondly, he discusses the possibility that some of the learning effects that he has just discussed will pass straight into the market without their instigators being compensated by the market. The problem of externalities leads to a divergence of private from social rates of return.

### 5.5 Paul Romer (1986)

Apart from some forerunners, what its proponents call the 'new' or 'endogenous' growth theory starts in 1986 with a paper by Paul Romer. He too identified technical change as the driving force, the prime mover, of the growth of the social product, or more precisely, of the product per capita. The real problem of growth theory is to explain how it comes about. Romer wants to endogenise the development of Solow's A. Technical progress, so his argument goes, is systematically created in the R&D departments of firms. He proposes to treat firms as consisting of two departments and ascribe to each their own production function for what they create: alongside the production department there appears the R&D department. 'Knowledge', new 'information', 'new industrial designs' are generated in the latter. Quite evidently this involves 'blueprints', propositional knowledge, and it is implied that all newly-created knowledge finds actual economic application, that is, invention and innovation are one and the same thing. However, knowledge that has been

{76}------------------------------------------------

created but which does not succeed in the market at the time it is available is not treated as a component of relevant knowledge. The open question of choice of technique is not dealt with. There is just as little discussion of how invention is to be distinguished from innovation as there is of the question whether an invention that gains no acceptance at its current price might actually have some chance at other prices in the course of its development, or to be selected following the invention of other, complementary inventions.<sup>14</sup>

Romer assumes  $N$  firms, which all produce exactly the same product. Over time, the number of firms remains constant, suggesting (in Marshall's terms) an analysis of the short run. Nevertheless, Romer takes his analysis to be secular. The production function of the  $i^{\text{th}}$  firm is

$$Y_i = F(k_i, K, x_i) \quad (5.2)$$

with  $k_i$  as the stock of knowledge actually available to the firm,  $x_i$  as the vector of the firm's other factors of production (such as labour and land) and  $K$  as the total stock of knowledge in the economy. Romer (1986; 1019) assumes that 'factors other than knowledge are in fixed supply'. This means that knowledge is the sole accumulable factor of production or capital good employed in the creation of the consumption good. Romer assumes that the function  $F()$  is concave in respect of  $k_i$  and justifies this assumption in the following way: 'Without this assumption, a competitive equilibrium will not exist in general' (ibid., p. 215).

'So what!' the undisciplined reader might interject; 'so such an equilibrium does not exist. My stylised experience of the world tells me different anyway.' But what follows materially from the assumption of concavity? It quite obviously only makes sense if the knowledge in question is a magnitude that is cardinally measurable. But Romer retreats into silence on this issue of the cardinal metric of the 'stock of knowledge'.<sup>15</sup>

He goes on to assume implicitly that new knowledge is not the enemy of old knowledge. Already existing knowledge is not subject to 'moral obsolescence' (Marx) in the way that, as we know, capital goods and human qualifications embodying knowledge produced in the past are shouldered aside (from the market) by knowledge produced in the present. There is, in Schumpeter's words, no such 'creative destruction'.<sup>16</sup> New knowledge rather adds itself to old knowledge. Romer finally assumes without any further discussion that

$$K = \sum_i k_i. \quad (5.3)$$

The total social stock of technological and organisational knowledge,  $K$ , is assumed to be an element of the production function of each individual firm whose impact works as it were behind the backs of the agents involved. Romer seeks in this way to express the idea that privately-created knowledge cannot be kept entirely private, or successfully patented in perpetuity, but partially assumes the character of a public good: the criterion of excludability applies only temporarily to knowledge, and that of rivalry in consumption does not apply at all. With  $K$  as an element of the production function of every firm Romer introduces a positive externality into the system. While the returns to scale in respect of the inputs  $k_i$  and  $x_i$  controlled by the single firm are assumed to be constant for every firm, they are assumed to be increasing in respect of  $k_i$  and  $K$ . Private and social rate of return in the accumulation of knowledge therefore diverge from each other and reflect the circumstance that knowledge is a quasi-public good.

{77}------------------------------------------------

The creation of knowledge and the associated positive externality therefore prove themselves to be the genuine motor of the growth of social product per head. The accumulation of capital, so the message goes, is the accumulation of knowledge. In an economy in which employment tends to remain constant (in Romer's model: where the labour input aggregated over  $N$  firms does not alter), as was in the recent past true of many developed nations, growth is related exclusively to the accumulation of knowledge. Seen from this perspective, 'Solow's residual' vanishes.

Can knowledge really be aggregated like that? This would be true if it were of a uniform quality and so only quantitatively distinguishable; or if it were of different quality and a generally-accepted key existed through which different qualities could be reduced to a common quantitative measure. Here we might recall the Classical economists, whose main representatives, Smith and Ricardo, reduced differing qualities of labour to a unitary quality through a system of wage differentials thought to be relatively stable. Is there an analogous and stable conversion key for different kinds of knowledge as well? Many authors have registered their doubt on this score. For Boulding (1966: 2) the absence of a 'unit' of knowledge and the intrinsic heterogeneity of its substance is why one has so much trouble trying to apply economic categories such as price or commodity to knowledge. Arrow likewise emphasises the 'qualitative distinctness of different items of information' (1969: 30).

But let us proceed with the examination of the Romer model. The second department of every firm, hence also the  $i^{\text{th}}$ , creates new knowledge. This obviously exclusively involves propositional knowledge that is available for debate, but which, when available (we must assume), immediately mutates into procedural knowledge. Romer describes the creation of knowledge through what he calls 'research technology', which is a kind of production function of the R&D department:

$$\dot{k}_i = G(I_i, k_i). \quad (5.4)$$

$\dot{k}$

Here  $\dot{k}_i$  is the change in firm-specific knowledge over time,  $k_i$  the current stock of this knowledge, and  $I_i$  the amount of resources invested by the firm in the generation of new knowledge, measured in units of the consumption good produced by the firm's production department. This is based on the idea 'that there is a trade-off between consumption today and knowledge that can be used to produce more consumption tomorrow' (Romer 1986: 1015). It can be deduced from the functional signs  $F$  and  $G$  that Romer assumes that all  $N$  firms dispose of the same production function.

In the course of his discussion Romer stumbles on the fact, as already noted, that a steady-state equilibrium under conditions of increasing rates of return can only be defined if the marginal product of knowledge is restricted on the upside. Otherwise the system would be characterised by a secularly increasing rate of profit, and hence rate of growth. Romer uses an ad hoc assumption in regard to research technology to effect this restriction (ibid.: 1019).

Romer avoids the discussion of monopolistic elements – unlike Aghion and Howitt (1992) – and, ultimately, the development over time of the total number of firms by introducing the assumption that technical knowledge is equally distributed over all firms, that is, he assumes that  $k_i = K/N$ ,  $i = 1, 2, \dots, N$ . The  $N$  firms are therefore identical: at any moment of time they not only have at their disposal the same technical possibilities, but also the same 'quantity' of knowledge. This assumption places the aggregation expressed in equation (5.3) on the stock of firm-specific knowledge in an especially harsh light. If all firms have at their disposal the same knowledge, how can the total stock of knowledge in the economy be greater than the stock of knowledge at the disposal of each individual firm? And how then in this case can, as in equation (5.2), a positive externality arise? The first objection can also be made, if in a somewhat weaker form, where there is

{78}------------------------------------------------

a differential in knowledge between firms, to the extent that the firms concerned share some elements of knowledge among themselves.<sup>17</sup> Romer's conception of knowledge in the economy as a whole suffers from serious multiple-counting, and is also for this reason problematic.

Moreover, also problematic is the failure to distinguish different kinds of knowledge. It is assumed that all firms share the results of the R&D of those firms especially active in R&D through the assumed externality, but there is no discussion of the costs to which industrial espionage and so forth give rise to and how the blueprints obtained translate into 'know how to do'.

Romer's model is suggestive and has a particular aesthetic appeal, but on close examination one asks oneself where, with all the refinements of modelling involved, exactly the gain is for an understanding of the process of growth and development.

### 5.6 Robert Lucas (1988)

Two years after Romer's paper Robert Lucas published his own contribution to discussion of endogenous growth in which the formation of 'human capital' was the object of the analysis, not the systematic creation of new technical knowledge. Like Romer before him, Lucas looked to a positive externality to generate growth of social product per head in his model.

Before we go any further, it should be noted that Adam Smith saw a strict analogy between a lasting capital good and trained labour:

A man educated at the expence of much labour and time to any of those employments which require extraordinary dexterity and skill, may be compared to one of those expensive machines.

(Smith WN, I.x.b. 6)<sup>18</sup>

Smith also understood that the quality of a labourer depended both on his training and his accumulated experience. We have dealt above with this second aspect in discussing the work of Kenneth Arrow; we now need to discuss the first aspect.

Drawing on Uzawa (1965), Lucas assumes that agents have a choice between two ways of using their time (apart from a given and constant amount of leisure per unit of time):<sup>19</sup> they can either contribute to the ongoing production of the consumption good, which can also be used as capital good,  $Y$ , or increase their personal human capital,  $h$ , in the education sector. The allocation of time between the two alternatives determines the growth of the economy. A shift in the time budget in favour of the accumulation of human capital in one period depresses the period output of the consumption good and thereby present consumption. The subsequent rise in the stock of human capital increases the future output of the consumption good, and hence future consumption. The formation of human capital therefore takes its place alongside the formation of physical capital as an instrument to decide the intertemporal stream of consumption. Since utility is taken to derive only from consuming, the 'representative' (immortal) agent shapes the stream of consumption over time in an optimal, utility-maximising way.

The externality at issue states: the greater the amount of human capital in total in a society, the more productive each and every member of that society is. This is expressed in the following production function for the multipurpose good:

$$Y = AK^\beta(uhN)^{1-\beta}h^{\gamma} \quad (5.5)$$

$K$  here denotes physical capital, and  $uhN$  the 'effective quantity of labour employed'. This is equal to the number of workers,  $N$ , multiplied with the share of their time that they spend in production,  $u$ ,

{79}------------------------------------------------

multiplied by the human capital per worker,  $h$ , expressing the achieved stock of capabilities and skills. If we ignore the final term in the production function, we have a conventional Cobb-Douglas function with constant returns to scale. It differs from the production function employed by Solow since we are now dealing with two accumulable factors: physical and human capital, and no longer with one accumulable factor (capital) and one non-accumulable factor (labour). Whereas in the Solow model (without exogenous technical progress) increasing capital intensity is necessarily related to a falling marginal productivity of capital, and hence a falling rate of profit, in Lucas's model rising capital intensity,  $K/N$ , does not necessarily have this effect. This is especially not the case if the quantity of labour employed  $uhN$  moves together with the rise in the physical stock of capital on account of rising human capital per head.

As expression (5.5) shows, Lucas quite clearly assumes that human capital is cardinally measurable:

By an individual's 'human capital' I will mean . . . simply his general skill level, so that a worker with human capital  $h(t)$  is the productive equivalent of two workers with  $\frac{1}{2}h(t)$  each, or a half-time worker with  $2h(t)$ .

(Lucas 1988: 17)

But he says nothing about the cardinal measure of the general skill level. Instead, we can read that human capital is an 'admittedly unobservable force' (p. 35). How, though, can something which is unobserved be specified quantitatively? Interestingly, Lucas says the same thing about physical capital:

Physical capital, too, is best viewed as a force, not directly observable, that we postulate in order to account in a unified way for certain things we can observe.

(Ibid.: 36; the first two emphases are mine)<sup>20</sup>

Both (hence all) of the factors of production in this model are consequently unobservable! Despite all of this, Lucas seems to be quite certain that each of the two 'forces' dispose of their own cardinal measure. Otherwise he would have to stop at this point, for if things were any different, there would be no sense in talking of rates of return, marginal productivity and average productivity in respect of the two factors etc. (see also Kurz 1997 and Steedman 2003).

Another advance on the Solow model concerns the third term in (5.5), the positive externality. The term  $h^*$  is the average amount of human capital per worker. Since Lucas however assumes that the average level for all  $N$  workers is the same,  $h = h^*$ . Nonetheless, he assumes that the representative agent has control over the development of  $K$  and  $h$  through the choice of consumption (per head)  $c$  as well as over the portion of time spent in production  $u$ . And so his calculation of optimality presumes constant returns to scale in production of the multipurpose good (the partial production elasticities are  $\beta$  and  $1 - \beta$ ), although for the society as a whole the partial production elasticity in relation to human capital is not equal to  $1 - \beta$ , but to  $1 - \beta + \gamma$ . Lucas insists that the accumulation of human capital is a 'social activity', 'involving groups of people in a way that has no counterpart in the accumulation of physical capital' (1988: 19). This gives rise to the following questions: with the assumed equal distribution of human capital among agents, how can there be a positive externality? In such a case, how can a person or a society benefit beyond that which this knowledge means in itself and for the individual person in respect of productivity? This objection is at the same level as that raised against the Romer model concerning the equal distribution of knowledge among firms in regard to multiple counting.<sup>21</sup>

It is supposed that human capital is appropriated in an education sector; Lucas seeks to capture this in the following simple expression:

{80}------------------------------------------------

$$\dot{h} = vh(1 - u). \quad (5.6)$$

$\dot{h}$

Here  $\dot{h}$  is the change in human capital over time and  $u$  is a positive constant. The equation can be regarded as a production function for human capital. Although Lucas says nothing directly, the knowledge here described as human capital is a mixture of propositional and procedural knowledge. In educational institutions such as schools and universities the emphasis is generally on the communication of propositional knowledge, although partly also procedural knowledge – one thinks of labs, vocational training, commercial colleges and polytechnics. Since the acquired human capital has an immediate impact upon the production of the consumption good, but no wider effects (for instance, through cumulative production in terms of the gains from specialisation), Lucas's  $h$  is clearly a catch-all variable in respect of the extension of economically-relevant knowledge. The resulting increase of productivity is 'embodied' not in new capital goods, but in better-trained workers.

If the number of agents (workers) does not alter, they all acquire human capital and physical capital according to the solution of their (identical) intertemporal utility maximisation problem. In long-run equilibrium both forms of capital yield under competitive conditions the same rate of interest.

We gain some interesting insights into the model if we ignore the externality ( $\gamma = 0$ ). In this case growth is still endogenous, as Lucas has emphasised. Contrary to a widespread view, the externality is therefore not responsible for the endogeneity of growth, although it is for rising income per capita in the steady state. More interesting perhaps is the finding (see Kurz and Salvadori 1998: 79–80) that for  $\gamma = 0$  the following values for the rate of profit in a steady state result:

$$r = v + \lambda \quad (5.7)$$

with  $\lambda$  as the exogenous rate of growth of the number of identical ('representative') agents,  $N$ , or the working and consuming population. There are again two things worth noting about this result: firstly, without the externality the rate of profit is given purely exogenously and is equal to the sum of the two parameters ( $v$  and  $\lambda$ ). Contrariwise the rate of growth is determined endogenously and is equal to

$$g = \frac{r - \rho}{\sigma} \quad (5.8)$$

with  $\rho$  as the rate of discount or time preference, and  $1/\sigma$  as the elasticity of substitution between

$\dot{K}$   $\dot{K}$

present and future consumption ( $1 \neq \sigma > 0$ ), with  $Y = c(t) + \frac{\dot{K}}{K}$ , in which  $\frac{\dot{K}}{K}$  is the derivative of  $K$  over time, i.e. represents savings and investment. It could be said that the relations that prevail in the Solow model are here turned on their head. In the Solow model the steady-state rate of growth was exogenous (and in the case of no technical progress equal to  $\lambda$ ), while the related rate of profit was endogenous:  $r = f'(k^*)$ , with  $f'$  as the first derivative of the intensive form of the production function in the place where capital intensity,  $k$ , assumes its steady-state value  $k^*$ . It could therefore be said that the Solow model involves a theory of exogenous (steady-state) growth and endogenous profitability, while the Lucas model involves, for  $\gamma = 0$ , a theory of exogenous profitability and endogenous growth.<sup>22</sup>

{81}------------------------------------------------

For our discussion here perhaps more interesting is the fact that the rate of profit depends upon an externally-given rate of population growth. This fact allows of one of the following two interpretations only: either 'population' growth means in the Lucas model that that each individual member of a numerically stable population grows at the rate  $\lambda$ , i.e. becomes 'bigger'. Or we must assume that another externality exists that Lucas has not mentioned: the costless cultural transmission of the given stock of human capital per head. In other words, for newly arriving agents the knowledge that already exists in society is a good that is transferred to them timelessly and costlessly. The newborn acquire it in their cradles, as it were. From the very beginning of their lives, they are just as 'representative' as their fellows who are already working. A remarkably smooth and silent process of osmosis is here at work in respect of knowledge!<sup>[23](#)</sup>

### 5.7 Paul Romer (1990)

In his later writings, Romer admitted to some conceptual weaknesses and logical inconsistencies in his original argument and sought to overcome these with reformulations. In this section, we deal with his paper of 1990 in which he sought to bring together several ideas and mechanisms. Here he (a) combined the idea of endogeneity of technical knowledge with (b) the role of human capital for growth as well as (c) a 'product-diversity' specification of physical capital. The formalisation of the last element was borrowed from the model of monopolistic competition in respect of consumption goods elaborated by Dixit and Stiglitz (1977).

Romer's model is based upon three premises (1990: S72). The first is that technical progress is the 'prime mover' of economic development. The second, that technical change is 'in large part' the outcome of intentional action on the part of agents reacting to market signals – the market signals lead to new knowledge being incorporated in goods that can be sold profitably. The third premise – the 'most fundamental premise' according to Romer – is that technical directions for the use of raw materials and other inputs are quite distinct to other goods. The development of an 'industrial design', or new economically exploitable knowledge, does create setting-up costs, but afterwards the knowledge can be used without incurring further costs and can be employed repeatedly. The setting-up costs are therefore a kind of fixed cost. Unlike human capital, design has no need of embodiment. Unlike many other goods, it is nonrival and only partially excludable.

'Growth is driven fundamentally by the accumulation of a partially excludable, nonrival input' (ibid.: S74) Knowledge, unlike human capital, can be accumulated without there being a per capita upper limit, and because of its incomplete excludability it leads to the phenomenon of spillovers. The presence of nonrival inputs unavoidably implies non-convexities.

The model has four basic sectors and four types of inputs. In the research sector, new knowledge is created through the use of human capital and the existing knowledge, which is taken to be freely accessible for research purposes. The outcome of the research process are new designs for intermediary products or capital goods which are produced in the second sector. Importantly, it is assumed that these capital goods neither depreciate technically nor become economically obsolete (ibid.: S85). The proportions of knowledge and human capital employed in the research sector are taken to be the same, independent of whichever design is being created. Designs are therefore not capable of distinction one from another as far as the input side goes. The assumption of nonrivalry of designs is therefore transferred into the much bolder assumption of the eternal use of intermediate products produced on their basis; once created each of these intermediate products is like an inexhaustible resource that permanently yields a return.<sup>[24](#)</sup> In the second sector, a number of monopolistic firms equal to the number of designs that are all of the same economic value produce by means of these designs as well as parts of the output of the third sector intermediate products. (The knowledge available in society at any one time is hence not excludable from research, while it is entirely so from the production of intermediate products.) The proportions in which designs and final product used as capital good are employed is everywhere the same, independent of whichever intermediate product is being currently produced. And so also intermediate products are not

{82}------------------------------------------------

distinguishable from one another from the input side. These intermediate products are finally employed in the third sector together with labour and human capital in order to produce the final product, which product is the sole consumption good and which also finds employment in the second sector as a capital good. Romer points out quite rightly that the dual employment of one and the same good introduces an 'extreme assumption':

Assuming that capital can be accumulated as forgone output is equivalent to assuming that capital goods are produced in a separate sector that has the same technology as the final-output sector.

(Ibid.: S80)

He does not state that he needs this extreme assumption so that he might retain the aggregate production function. (This assumption underlies the Solow model as well as that of Lucas.) This means that from the input side there is no way of distinguishing the consumption good from the relevant capital good. Romer finally assumes that the fourth sector – that of human capital formation – remains unactivated: the quantity of human capital in the system as a whole,  $H$ , is given and constant, exactly like the quantity of simple labour,  $L$  (ibid.: S79).<sup>25</sup> Since he defines human capital as 'a distinct measure of the cumulative effect of activities such as formal education and on-the-job training' (ibid.: S79), we can say in respect of our above discussion that his conception seeks to encompass both the Lucas and the Arrow learning effects. But since  $H$  is independent of the other state variables of the model there is an implicit ignoring of the second type of learning effect.

The four types of input are accordingly simple labour, human capital, capital and an index of the technical level, or knowledge. If we consider the interconnection of production between the three sectors we can say that knowledge and human capital are used, either directly or indirectly, in the production of all goods; while intermediate products and the consumption or capital good are used, directly or indirectly, only in the production of themselves. Romer maintains a remarkable silence on the question of how knowledge might be measured, although according to his own view this factor is the real basis of economic development. As we shall see below, he characterises the 'amount' of knowledge available at any one time simply with a natural number,  $A$ .

The principal novelty of this model, by comparison with that of 1986 as well as those others considered here, is that he seeks to take account of an ever greater variety of products, in which a growing amount of knowledge is incorporated. This aspect needs to be recognised in any serious analysis of the processes of development and growth. This was at any rate the case in the period of classical economics, of Marx, Walras, the Austrians and Schumpeter.<sup>26</sup> However, Romer immediately restricts the analysis to diversification on the input side, and holds fast to the assumption that there is only one final product. He writes:

The unusual feature of the production technology assumed here is that it disaggregates capital into an infinite number of distinct types of producer durables. . . . Only a finite number of these potential inputs, the ones that have already been invented and designed, are available for use at any time.

(Ibid.: S80)

The final product is assumed to be produced according to the following extended Cobb–Douglas function:

$$Y(H_Y, L, x) = H_Y^\alpha L^\beta \sum_{i=1}^{\infty} x_i^{1-\alpha-\beta} \quad (5.9)$$

{83}------------------------------------------------

with  $H_Y$  as the human capital employed in the relevant industry,  $L$  as the number of workers there

$$\sum_{i=1}^{\infty} x_i^{1-\alpha-\beta}$$

employed and as the use of intermediate products, that is, specific capital goods (as distinct to final product also used as capital good). At a given point in time there is only a finite number  $A$  of intermediate products, so that  $x_i = 0$  for all  $i > A$ . The output of the final product is therefore an additive, separable function of the different intermediate products.<sup>27</sup>

How heterogeneous is knowledge and its embodiment in an unlimited number of intermediate products in Romer's model? In the description given above of the assumptions related to the technique of production, we have seen that in not one case can any conclusions be drawn from the input side concerning the product created – whether it be a design, an intermediate product or a consumption (or capital) good. In particular, there is only the appearance of the implied diversity of produced means of production. Since in their creation there enter only amounts of the final product that are withheld from consumption, they embody only this final product, and since none of the intermediate products ever becomes economically obsolete, they embody it exactly in proportion to the consumption foregone. The stock of capital is therefore equal to the cumulative abstention from consumption. Romer confirms this in stating that it is

useful to define an accounting measure of total capital  $K$  as cumulative forgone output. . . . Because it takes  $\eta$  units of forgone consumption to create one unit of any type of durable, this accounting measure  $K$  is related to the durable goods that are actually used in production by the rule

$$K = \eta \sum_{i=1}^{\infty} x_i = \eta \sum_{i=1}^A x_i.$$

(Ibid.: S82)

Different capital goods can therefore be quite easily aggregated, that is, without a knowledge of their relative prices and hence the rate of interest.<sup>28</sup> Without significant loss in the generality of the argument we can set  $\eta = 1$  and assume in addition that each of the intermediate products represents the same amount of capital, ie.

$$x_1 = x_2 = \dots = x_A = x$$

From this, it follows that<sup>29</sup>

$$K = Ax \tag{5.10}$$

Romer is aware of the problems involved in the bold assumptions that he has piled up, one following another. He sweeps them aside with this comment:

Nonetheless, the general results here should be [sic] robust to more careful modeling [sic] of the nature of the interaction between different specialized producer durables.

(Ibid.: S85)

What makes him think that the results are robust? Knowledge accumulated with regard to the properties of models with (truly) heterogeneous capital goods does not support his optimism.

{84}------------------------------------------------

The system is driven along by an ever-increasing  $A$ , the number of designs embodied in intermediate products. In the final analysis, growing technological knowledge is responsible for the growth in social product per head. The beneficent consequences of knowledge unfold in various ways. On the one hand, an increasing  $A$  raises, via the intermediate products entering into the production of the final good, the productivity of the inputs  $HY$  und  $L$  there employed. On the other hand, there is a direct and positive relation, thanks to the assumption of non-excludability in research, between the existing 'total stock of designs and knowledge' (ibid.: S83),  $A$ , and the productivity of the human capital employed in research,  $H_A$ . Romer assumes the following research technology, drawing upon his 1986 paper:

$$\dot{A} = \delta H_A A \quad (5.11)$$

with  $\delta$  as a positive parameter. Clearly this equation only makes sense if cardinal measures exist for both  $H_A$  and  $A$ . Then, and only then, can the rate of growth of knowledge be defined as  $\delta$   $H_A$ . Romer comments on this condition as follows:

Linearity in  $A$  is what makes unbounded growth possible, and in this sense, unbounded growth is more like an assumption than a result of the model.

(Ibid.: S84; emphasis added)

Since the total quantity of human capital,  $H$ , is given exogenously, it is a matter of the division of this quantity among its alternative uses: in research or in the production of the final good. In equilibrium this distribution (and hence  $H_A$ ) is fixed.

The formalisation of 'product diversity' here suggested in respect of intermediate products exhibits some family resemblance with the concept of the 'superiority of more roundabout processes of production' put forward by representatives of the Austrian School, and especially by Eugen von Böhm-Bawerk in his interpretation of Smith's conception of an increasing social division of labour.<sup>30</sup> As is well known, Böhm-Bawerk sought to resolve the problem of the heterogeneity (and increasing diversity) of capital goods by means of the conception of the 'average period of production'. With the assistance of this conception he sought to be able to aggregate heterogeneous capital goods for individual industries, or for the economy as a whole, independently of their prices (and hence from the given distribution of income) into a scalar magnitude, the 'quantity of capital'. The rate of interest (or the general rate of profit) could then be derived from the relative scarcity of this quantity of capital, more or less the triumphant conclusion of the theory. But Böhm-Bawerk's efforts were flawed, as already Knut Wicksell pointed out (see Kurz 2000): apart from some uninteresting special cases, there is no possibility of ascertaining consistently the quantity of capital in one sector or in the economy as a whole in this way (see also Kurz and Salvadori 1995: Ch. 14).<sup>31</sup>

Looked at from this perspective, how does Romer deal with the problem of the variety, and increasing variety, of capital goods? One could loosely say that he replaces Böhm-Bawerk's 'average period of production' with a kind of 'absolute period of production', his  $A$ . Economic development means that the absolute production period becomes ever longer, that is,  $A$  becomes ever larger. But since he also assumes that every intermediate product has an eternal life, those which were created at an early stage do not have to be constantly replaced. This is the second difference with respect to Böhm-Bawerk, and closely linked to the first: while the latter only dealt

{85}------------------------------------------------

with circulating capital, Romer allows only for extremely long-lived – perennial – capital. As we have seen  $K = Ax$ . Romer invites the reader to imagine that  $A$  is the number of 'designs'. But beyond Romer's bold assumptions – in my opinion, hardly less bold than those of Böhm-Bawerk – it remains unclear what the sum (or the integral) of such 'blueprints' is supposed to mean. Quite obviously, 'apples and oranges' are added together here without the intermediary of prices. The purpose of the enterprise is clear enough: one tries to avoid at all cost to deal with the awkward problem of the heterogeneity of capital.

At the beginning of this paper, we cited Romer's view that the whole of neoclassical growth theory, old and new, is made up of a series of short cuts which 'do violence to the complexity of economic activity'. This is of course true for any kind of theory. The question is not whether such forcible means can, or might be, applied; but rather which forcible means are appropriate. In particular, we need to ask whether the assumptions upon which a theory is based have in important respects to reflect the reality, which the theory proposes to analyse, or whether this requirement is dispensable? According to the instrumentalist methodology, to which many neoclassical theorists adhere (back to the early (Walrasian) Schumpeter (1908) and especially vehemently argued by Friedman (1953)), even serious violation of this condition is no ground for the rejection of a theory. The fate of a theory is decided not by the degree to which its assumption mimick reality, but by the realism of the results which are gained from its use – its prognostic power. It does not matter how bold, contrived or even bizarre the individual building blocks of a theory might be thought, all that counts is the end result: is the theory, or one of its models, capable, on the basis of the assumptions made, of providing a sufficiently good explanation through an ex-post prognosis of economic development expressed in a collection of 'stylised facts'?

The explanatory or prognostic capacity of growth theory, especially of 'new' growth theory, alas!, is not that impressive: see for discussion of this Mankiw et al. (1992), Temple (1999) and Helpman (2004). But even if the empirical test had been more successful than has turned out so far, is there any basis to the assumption that the theory might provide a convincing 'explanation' of the phenomenon in question? This is doubtful, since the instrumentalist position is hardly tenable. The good statistical fit of approaches based upon contrived premises is possibly only accidental, or proves – as in the Solow model – on closer examination as no more than the hardly surprising result of the empirical verification of an accounting identity (see Felipe and McCombie 2005).<sup>32</sup>

### 5.8 Martin L. Weitzman (1998)

In his paper 'Recombinant Growth' Weitzman intends 'to get inside the black box of innovation and pull out an explicit model of knowledge production' (1998: 331).<sup>33</sup> This is no easy task, for 'what is required, ultimately, is some theory of the creative process itself' (ibid.: 332). He conceives of the generation of new 'ideas' as a function of reconfigured old ideas. The model for the approach chosen is the agricultural research station, in which a greater diversity of plants is produced by pollination of different plant types. The model that he develops shows how knowledge can build upon itself through a combinatory feedback process.<sup>34</sup>

According to Weitzman, the results gained have important implications for growth theory. In his view, the limits to growth lie not so much in the capacity to create new ideas, as is assumed by Romer (1990) and also Aghion and Howitt (1992), but instead in the capacity to mould the wealth of new ideas into useable form.

Weitzman's basic idea recalls one of Adam Smith's formulations. In [Chapter 1](#) of Book I of *The Wealth of Nations*, 'Of the Division of Labour', the chapter in which Smith sought to identify the causes of the progress of productivity in a society, he notes that improved and new machinery is owed not only to 'learning by using', as Nathan Rosenberg would say, but:

{86}------------------------------------------------

All the improvements in machinery . . . have by no means been the inventions of those who had occasion to use the machines. Many improvements have been made by the ingenuity of the makers of the machines, when to make them became the business of a peculiar trade; and some by that of those who are called philosophers or men of speculation, whose trade it is, not to do anything, but to observe every thing; and who, upon that account, are often capable of combining together the powers of the most distant and dissimilar objects. In the progress of society, philosophy or speculation becomes, like every other employment, the principal or sole trade and occupation of a particular class of citizens.

(Smith WN, I.i. 9; emphasis added)

Smith clearly articulates the idea of combining existing fragments of knowledge as a means of gaining new fragments. The same idea can be found in Schumpeter's conception of 'new combinations' (1912). It then assumes new form in Boulding's idea of the 'tree of knowledge' (1956: 95).<sup>35</sup> Economic development is comparable with the growth of this tree. At each stage of development, there are quite particular alternatives for the future course of development. All of them build on what has gone before. One specific technical innovation, for example the car, presupposes numerous previous technical developments. The process is path-dependent.

The underlying idea is distinguished by Weitzman from other kinds of knowledge-based growth that we have encountered above. He has especially in mind Romer's concept of a 'research technology' when he writes: "New ideas" are simply taken to be some exogenously determined function of "research effort" in the spirit of a humdrum conventional relationship between inputs and outputs.' This conception is said to be quite misleading:

It seems to me that something fundamentally different is involved here. When research effort is applied, new ideas arise out of existing ideas in some kind of cumulative interactive process that intuitively seems somewhat different from prospecting for petroleum. To me, the research process has at its center a sort of pattern-finding or combinatoric feel.

(Weitzman, 1998; 332)

Weitzman does not therefore opt in favour of using a conventional production function as a representation of the process of knowledge generation, but employs instead combinatorial mathematics to grasp what seems to be going on in the 'black box'. As noted already, he takes his inspiration from the example of an agricultural research station where existing 'idea-cultivars' are combined with each other in pairs so that new 'hybrid ideas' might be produced. ('Cultivar' is an acronym for 'cultivated variety'). If A is the number of idea-cultivars, then the corresponding number of different binary pairs that can be created from A is equal to  $C_2(A)$ . It can be shown that

$$C_2(A) = \frac{A!}{(A-2)!2!}$$

If for example A = 4, then  $C_2(4) = 6$ ; if A = 5, then  $C_2(5) = 10$ ; if A = 6, then  $C_2(6) = 15$ , and so on.

Weitzman's thoughts turn on this idea. It states that an expansion that is based on previously untried combinatorial reconfigurations of existing elements exceeds exponential growth. If all fragments of knowledge arising from recombination were actually used; if each recombination would require the same time; and if all fragments of knowledge had the same economic weight, the rate of growth would over time become ever greater. Empirically, however, we do not observe such a situation. There must then be elements that hinder such a course of development.

{87}------------------------------------------------

To make economic use of new ideas it is necessary to engage in costly effort related to development and marketing. The extent of such effort limits the 'harvest' gained. Weitzman assumes fixed proportions and ultimately concludes that the rate of growth of actually realised new ideas,  $g_A$ , is the minimum of two rates. Let  $\pi$  be the exogenously-given probability of success in rendering new hybrid ideas into a useful form,  $s$  the exogenously-given rate of saving or investment, that is, the share of all resources at the disposal of the recombination process, and  $\beta$  an exogenous productivity parameter which denotes how the invested resources employed do actually increase output. Then

$$g_A(t) = \frac{A(t+1) - A(t)}{A(t)} = \text{Min} \left\{ \pi \left( \frac{C_2[A(t)] - C_2[A(t-1)]}{A(t)} \right), s\beta \right\}. \quad (5.12)$$

According to Weitzman, there is reason to assume that the first rate to the right of the second equality sign is greater than the second, which is why  $s\beta$  proves to be a limit.

The limiting potential to generate new ideas here increases quadratically in ideas while the capacity to process new ideas increases linearly; the ultimate constraint on economic expansion is therefore linear.

(Weitzman 1998: 343)

As Weitzman emphasises, interestingly the model with a recombinant innovation process leads to a result similar to that of the so-called Harrod-Domar model, the model that Solow had originally wanted to supersede through his neoclassical approach:

In the interpretation here, everything comes full circle to steady-state growth rates being linearly proportional to aggregate savings, with the Solow model representing the limiting case of a very small constant of proportionality. . . .

The underlying reason is that the combinatoric power of a recombinant growth process essentially overwhelms the potential diminishing returns of a neoclassical production function, thereby resurrecting in the limit the growth dynamics of the linear case.

(Ibid.: 354-5)

Like Romer (1986, 1990), Weitzman does not put forward any cardinal measure of knowledge, but is content to give its current size by a natural number. But if he is to move on to the expansion of per capita social product he has to assume the cardinal measurability of knowledge. He implies that all elements of knowledge have an equal weight.

Weitzman's principle interest lies in the path of balanced growth. The long waves of economic development that Schumpeter, prompted by the studies of Kondratiev and Spiethoff, treated as the substantial feature of the technological and organisational dynamic of capitalist economies and believed himself capable of diagnosing would in the modelling framework here presuppose either that the rate of growth of designs, or economically-realised hybrid ideas, or their specific economic weight, were liable to cyclical fluctuations.

### 5.9 Growth, population growth and steady states

In the Solow model without exogenous technical change labour is the sole limiting factor on long-run steady-state growth. The marginal productivity of capital and hence the rate of profit fall if the rate of accumulation of physical capital exceeds the exogenously-given rate of growth of the

{88}------------------------------------------------

working population. This mechanism was set aside in numerous contributions to the 'new' growth theory. This could happen in many ways (see Kurz and Salvadori 1998). The effect is however always the same: labour no longer plays the role of a limiting factor upon growth. In the most simple cases all factors that cannot be accumulated, among them simple labour, are just set aside, and only factors that can be accumulated are dealt with. These are either subsumed entirely into the one factor 'capital', as in the most simple versions of the AK model, or one distinguishes physical from human capital, the latter being produced by means of itself and the multipurpose good on the basis of a linear homogeneous production function. Lucas adds to all this, as we have seen, a positive externality associated with the formation of human capital. This has the effect of making the income per head grow faster than it otherwise would. The effect of increasing productivity can, however, derive from other activities that have nothing to do with the formation of human capital, as in Arrow's 'learning by doing' and Romer's endogenisation of technical progress (or even to some degree, in Weitzman's model). The external effects assumed in Romer's model compensate for, or even overcompensate for, the tendency for growth to slow down with the growth in the relative scarcity of non-accumulable factors as compared with those that can be accumulated. In fact, the postulated external effect has to be given an upper limit to avoid the implausible result of a constantly rising rate of growth over time.

In all of these models the factor labour is no longer a limit on growth: the accumulation of education, knowledge and ideas removes this limitation. Contrariwise, a more rapidly growing population is no guarantee of faster growth per head. As Romer states: 'The correct measure of scale is not population but human capital.' And: 'Growth seems to be correlated with the degree of integration into worldwide markets but not closely related to population size.' (1990: S78, S98).

But there is a view, which differs fundamentally from this one. Among others, it is represented by Charles Jones. Even this point of view resorts to the (global) discovery of new ideas as the motor of growth. Ideas arise in people, and this leads to the idea that the number of living persons is one of the defining magnitudes on the production of ideas, in extreme cases, the sole such magnitude. According to Jones (2002), the growth in the output of ideas is the greater, the more quickly the population grows. By more or less directly coupling the rate of technical progress to the rate of population growth one derives an endogenous growth model with Solow-type properties. In fact, Jones comes to the conclusion in one of his models that on the path of equilibrium expansion

$$g_y = \gamma n \quad (5.13)$$

with  $g_y$  the rate of growth of the social product per worker employed,  $n$  as the exogenous and constant rate of growth in the number of agents, and  $\gamma$  as a magnitude independent of several other (positive) parameters in the model (Jones, 2002: 224).

Empirical cross-sectional analyses of many countries have however shown there to be a negative correlation between the growth of income per head and that of the population as a whole. Jones sees no problem here: as also in the traditional neoclassical Solow model, one has to distinguish between (short run) transitional dynamics, and (long run) effects due to a change in the level of population growth. The expression above relates to the steady-state, while empirical studies are said to reflect the transitional dynamic of the neoclassical growth model (ibid.: 225). If for instance the rate of growth of the world population rises from one level to another, then in the short-run that means the human capital per head falls, with negative consequences for the rate of growth of the per capita product. Jones, in his empirical study for the period 1950–1993 sees the USA (and other developed countries) on a transitional path to balanced growth. As proof for this interpretation, he cites the fact that during the period in question the intensity of research has intensified much more strongly than income per head (p. 226). Correspondingly, the rate of growth of income per head is greater in the transitional than in the steady-state phase.

{89}------------------------------------------------

We make no comment on this speculation, but simply counter it with the speculation of another representative of new growth theory. Before we do so, a remark is called for. Up until the early 1970s the time horizon in intertemporal equilibrium models – and all of the growth models dealt with here fall into this class – was considered finite, and hence assumed to be arbitrary. The introduction of an infinite time horizon turned out to be of critical importance (see Burgstaller 1994: 43–8). This drove the analysis unavoidably in the direction of steady-state analysis. This was stated clearly in Lucas's paper. He emphasised that

For any initial capital  $K(0) > 0$ , the optimal capital-consumption path  $(K(t), c(t))$  will converge to the balanced path asymptotically. That is, the balanced path will be a good approximation to any actual path 'most' of the time . . . [T]his is exactly the reason why the balanced path is interesting to us.

(Lucas 1988: 11)

Lucas therefore advocated a return from general intertemporal analysis to steady-state analysis, and in fact in his paper we find only a study of the equilibrium path in the long run. How one is supposed to know whether one is on a long run path, or in a transitional state, is a further mystery of this difficult field of research, replete as it is with mystery.

### 5.10 Concluding remarks

According to Michael Ruse, the history of the reception of Darwin's *Origin of the Species* was characterised by two major trends, a bad one and a good one. The bad ran back to Herbert Spencer and sought to translate the theory of evolution into a doctrine of progress – from the weak and the bad to the strong and the good. The main proponent of the good branch in the reception was Darwin himself. He saw evolution as 'a directionless process, going nowhere rather slowly' (Ruse 1988: 97).

If we briefly summarise the above discussion in the light of this assessment, the following can perhaps be said:

- 1 There is progress in growth theory, but for the most part, if not exclusively, it relates to the formalisation of not implausible ideas that have all circulated among economists for some time. The formalisation of some of these ideas is itself anything other than plausible.
- 2 In part, formalisation brings greater clarity, especially where the interaction of various mechanisms is concerned. At the same time it is merciless in showing up the strict limits of such formalisation. The mathematical effort involved is often disappointing when compared to the results obtained. Much of this has to do with the restrictions arising from the centrality of equilibrium, where the phenomena in question have to be fitted into the chosen neoclassical framework.
- 3 What is striking, not to say irritating, is the carelessness with which the central conceptions of new growth theory, such as knowledge or human capital, are dealt with. It is assumed without any argument that these factors are capable of cardinal measurement, and wherever conceptual precision is lacking, mysterious 'forces' are invoked.
- 4 Heterogeneity and growing diversity in the world of knowledge and of goods do not command widespread popularity, and where some allowance seems to be made for this, closer scrutiny shows that this is more apparent than real.
- 5 The evolution of growth theory is perhaps not an entirely directionless process, but even in the few pages of this essay not a few circularities could be detected. The idea that the development of economic theory is a teleological process moving ever forwards, and never backwards, is naïve.

{90}------------------------------------------------

6 No matter what might otherwise be agreed about the process of development of growth theory, this process is slow, very slow. The great fuss made occasionally about supposedly disciplinary breakthroughs is hardly ever warranted.

7 How great is the progress in the understanding of development and growth since, for example, Adam Smith, and by what cardinal standard might we measure it? And how much less does the average historian of theory know about the nature of such theory compared with the average theorist?

Questions, questions! As always, Goethe has the answer. We can read the following in his Maxims and Reflections:

Theories are usually overhastiness on the part of impatient understanding, seeking to be quickly rid of phenomena and putting in their place images, concepts, often even only words. One senses, one even sees, that it is only an expedient; but does not passion and the spirit of partisanship always love expedience? And quite right, because they have need of it so much. (Goethe, 1953, p. 440, No. 548)
